H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}=SH_{\mathcal{O}}S
and
F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}=SF_{\mathcal{O}}S
. We also get easily that

•

E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
is of degree 2,
H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
is of degree 0 and
F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
is of degree -2;

•

\langle Xu,v\rangle+\langle u,Xv\rangle=0
for all
X\in\{E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}\}
and all
u,v\in V({\mathcal{O}})
;

•

[H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}]=2E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
[H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}]=-2F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
[E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}]=H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
, in other words,
(E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},H^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}})
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded Jacobson-Morozov triple;

•

the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}:V({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V({\mathcal{O}})
given by

\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},i}={E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}}|_{V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})}:V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{i+2}({\mathcal{O}})\quad\text{for all $i\in{\mathcal{I}}$ such that $(i+2)\in{\mathcal{I}}$},

is such that
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
is an orthogonal representation.

From our construction, we get that
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
and
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
are isomorphic relative to the orthogonal group with the isomorphism given by

S:(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}}).

We will now show that these two orthogonal representations
(V_{\mathcal{O}},\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
and
(V_{\mathcal{O}},\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
are not isomorphic relatively to the special orthogonal group.

Assume that
\theta:(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
,
\theta^{\prime}:(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
and
\theta^{\prime\prime}:(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
are orthogonal isomorphisms for these orthogonal representations. In other words, there are isomorphisms
\theta_{i}:V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})
(respectively
\theta^{\prime}_{i}:V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}:V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})
) for each
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
|i|\leq i_{1}
such that

•

\langle\theta_{i}(u),\theta_{-i}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
\langle\theta^{\prime}_{i}(u),\theta^{\prime}_{-i}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}
,
\langle\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}(u),\theta^{\prime\prime}_{-i}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}
) for all
u\in V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})
and
v\in V_{-i}({\mathcal{O}})
;

•

\theta_{i+2}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},i}=\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},i}\circ\theta_{i}
(respectively
\theta^{\prime}_{i+2}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},i}=\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},i}\circ\theta^{\prime}_{i}
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i+2}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},i}=\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},i}\circ\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}
)
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
|i|<i_{1}
;

•

\det(\theta_{i})\neq 0
and
\det(\theta_{-i})=(\det(\theta_{i}))^{-1}
(respectively
\det(\theta^{\prime}_{i})\neq 0
and
\det(\theta^{\prime}_{-i})=(\det(\theta^{\prime}_{i}))^{-1}
,
\det(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})\neq 0
and
\det(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{-i})=(\det(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))^{-1}
) for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
|i|\leq i_{1}
.

Lemma 2.21
.

With the above notation, we have that

(a)

\det(\theta_{0})=1
;

(b)

\det(\theta^{\prime}_{0})=1
;

(c)

\det(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0})=-1
.

Proof.

From our hypothesis, we have
i_{1}>|j_{1}|
.
V_{0}({\mathcal{O}})
has
\{v_{0}(b),v_{0}(\tau(b))\}
and we know how the elements
E_{\mathcal{O}}
and
E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
act on these vectors. Write

\left\{\begin{aligned} \theta_{0}(v_{0}(b))&=\alpha v_{0}(b)+\gamma v_{0}(\tau(b))\\
\theta_{0}(v_{0}(\tau(b)))&=\beta v_{0}(b)+\xi v_{0}(\tau(b))\end{aligned}\right.

\left\{\begin{aligned} \theta^{\prime}_{0}(v_{0}(b))&=\alpha^{\prime}v_{0}(b)+\gamma^{\prime}v_{0}(\tau(b))\\
\theta^{\prime}_{0}(v_{0}(\tau(b)))&=\beta^{\prime}v_{0}(b)+\xi^{\prime}v_{0}(\tau(b))\end{aligned}\right.

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} \theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}(v_{0}(b))&=\alpha^{\prime\prime}v_{0}(b)+\gamma^{\prime\prime}v_{0}(\tau(b))\\
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}(v_{0}(\tau(b)))&=\beta^{\prime\prime}v_{0}(b)+\xi^{\prime\prime}v_{0}(\tau(b))\end{aligned}\right.

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\xi,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbf{k}}
.
Because

\langle\theta_{0}(u),\theta_{0}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}},\ \langle\theta^{\prime}_{0}(u),\theta^{\prime}_{0}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}\text{ and }\langle\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}(u),\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}(v)\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}

for all
u,v\in V_{0}({\mathcal{O}})
, we get by considering the following pairs:
(u,v)=(v_{0}(b),v_{0}(b))
,
(v_{0}(\tau(b)),v_{0}(\tau(b)))
and
(v_{0}(b),v_{0}(\tau(b)))
, we will get easily that

•

2\alpha\gamma=0
,
2\alpha^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}=0
,
2\alpha^{\prime\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}=0
;

•

2\beta\xi=0
,
2\beta^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}=0
,
2\beta^{\prime\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}=0
;

•

\alpha\xi+\gamma\beta=1
,
\alpha^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}=1
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}=1
.

Note that
V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})
has dimension
1
and
\{v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\}
is a basis of
V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})
. Also we have that
\theta_{(|j_{1}|+2)}:V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})
,
\theta^{\prime}_{(|j_{1}|+2)}:V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})
and
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{(|j_{1}|+2)}:V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V_{(|j_{1}|+2)}({\mathcal{O}})
are isomorphisms. Thus
\theta(v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b))=\chi v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)
,
\theta^{\prime}(v_{(|j_{1}|+2)})=\chi^{\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)
and
\theta^{\prime\prime}(v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b))=\chi^{\prime\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)
with
\chi,\chi^{\prime},\chi^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbf{k}}
,
\chi\neq 0
,
\chi^{\prime}\neq 0
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
.

(a) We have

\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},0}\circ\theta_{0}=\theta_{(|j_{1}|+2)}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},0}

If we evaluate this expression on each of the basis vectors of
V_{0}({\mathcal{O}})
, we get

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto\alpha v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto\beta v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the left side map }

and

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto\chi v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto 0\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the right side map.}

Thus
\beta=0
and we get that
\alpha\xi=1
and
\det(\theta_{0})=1
.

(b) We have

\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},0}\circ\theta^{\prime}_{0}=\theta^{\prime}_{(|j_{1}|+2)}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},0}

If we evaluate this expression on each of the basis vectors of
V_{0}({\mathcal{O}})
, we get

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto\gamma^{\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto\xi^{\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the left side map }

and

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto 0\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto\chi^{\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the right side map.}

Thus
\gamma^{\prime}=0
and we get that
\alpha^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}=1
and
\det(\theta^{\prime}_{0})=1
.

(c) We have

\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{O}},0}\circ\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}=\theta^{\prime\prime}_{(|j_{1}|+2)}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},|j_{1}|}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},2}\circ\phi_{{\mathcal{O}},0}

If we evaluate this expression on each of the basis vectors of
V_{0}({\mathcal{O}})
, we get

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto\gamma^{\prime\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto\xi^{\prime\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the left side map }

and

\begin{cases}v_{0}(b)\mapsto\chi^{\prime\prime}v_{(|j_{1}|+2)}(b)\\
v_{0}(\tau(b))\mapsto 0\end{cases}\quad\text{ for the right side map.}

Thus
\xi^{\prime\prime}=0
and we get that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}=1
and
\det(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0})=-1
.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

So
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
and
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
are isomorphic relative to the orthogonal group, but not isomorphic relative to the special orthogonal group. Because the index of the special orthogonal subgroup in the orthogonal group is 2, we get that any representation
(V_{\mathcal{O}},\phi^{\prime\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
isomorphic relative to the orthogonal group to either isomorphic to
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
or
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
relative to the special orthogonal group. In other words, the orthogonal isomorphism class of
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
splits into two isomorphism classes relatively to the special orthogonal group.

Note it is easy to see that if
{\mathcal{O}}
is the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of the
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b=b(i_{1},j_{1},0)\in{\mathbf{B}}
such that either
i_{1}+j_{1}=0
or
i_{1}\geq j_{1}>0
or
0>i_{1}\geq j_{1}
, then the orthogonal isomorphism class of
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
does not split and is a unique special orthogonal group isomorphism class.

3.
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even.

3.1.

In this section,

•

m
is an integer
>0
;

•

G
is the connected component of the identity element of the group of automorphisms of a finite dimensional vector space
V
over
{\mathbf{k}}
preserving a fixed non-degenerate symmetric (respectively skew-symmetric) bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:V\times V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
, in other words
G=SO(V)
(respectively
G=Sp(V)
);

•

\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
, more precisely
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if
X:V\rightarrow V
is an endomorphism of
V
such that
\langle X(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle+\langle v_{1},X(v_{2}) \rangle=0
for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
;

•

V
is identified to its dual
V^{*}
by the isomorphism
\phi:V\rightarrow V^{*}
defined by
v\mapsto\phi_{v}
, where
\phi_{v}:V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
is
\phi_{v}(x)=\langle v,x\rangle
for all
x\in V
;

•

{\mathcal{I}}=\{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mid n\equiv 1\pmod{2},-2m<n<2m\}
;

•

\oplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}V_{i}
is a direct sum decomposition of
V
by vector subspaces
V_{i}\neq 0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that

–

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0
whenever
v\in V_{i}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{j}
and
i+j\neq 0
;

–

V_{-i}=V_{i}^{*}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
under the identification given by
\phi
above;

•

{\mathcal{B}}=\coprod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
is a basis of
V
such that each
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\{u_{i,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
is a basis of
V_{i}
for each
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\delta_{i}
is the dimension of
V_{i}
. Moreover these bases are such that, for
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
1\leq r\leq\delta_{i}
,
1\leq s\leq\delta_{j}
, we have

\langle u_{i,r},u_{j,s}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $i+j=0$, $r=s$ and $i>0$;}\\
\epsilon,&\text{if $i+j=0$, $r=s$ and $i<0$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

\iota:{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}\rightarrow G
is the homomorphism defined by
\iota(t)v=t^{i}v
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
v\in V_{i}
and
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
;

•

{\mathbf{B}}
is the set of
{\mathcal{I}}
-boxes;

•

{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
denote the set of
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
in
{\mathbf{B}}
;

•

\nu=|{\mathbf{B}}/\tau|=m(m+1)
;

Lemma 3.2
.

(a)

g\in G^{\iota}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified:

•

g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

the restriction
g_{|_{V_{i}}}=g_{i}
of
g
to
V_{i}
belongs to
GL(V_{i})
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

g_{-i}^{*}g_{i}=Id_{V_{i}}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

Here
g_{-i}^{*}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
is defined using the identification of
V_{i}
with
V_{-i}^{*}
given by
\phi
. So
\langle g_{-i}^{*}(u),v\rangle=\langle u,g_{-i}(v)\rangle
for all
u\in V_{i}
and
v\in V_{-i}
.

(b)

X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified:

•

X(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq(2m-1)
;

•

X_{-i}=-X_{i-2}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq 3
;

•

X_{-1}=-\epsilon X_{-1}^{T}
;

where
X_{i}
is the matrix of the linear transformation
X|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}
of the restriction of
X
to
V_{i}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{i+2}
and
X_{i}^{T}
is its transpose for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
with
i\neq(2m-1)
. Moreover the subset of elements

(X_{i})_{\begin{subarray}{l}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-1)\end{subarray}}\in\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-1)\end{subarray}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})\right),

such that the above conditions for the
X_{i}
are satisfied, is a subspace of

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-1)\end{subarray}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})

isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
.

(c)

Z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified:

•

Z(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

Z_{-i}=-Z_{i}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

where we denote
Z_{i}
is the matrix of the linear transformation
Z|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
the restriction of
Z
to
V_{i}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
Z_{i}^{T}
is its transpose for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. Moreover the subset of elements

(Z_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in\left(\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i})\right),

such that the above conditions for the
Z_{i}
are satisfied, is a subspace of

\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i})

isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.

(d)

\{(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}GL(V_{i})\mid g_{-i}=(g_{i}^{*})^{-1}\text{ for all $i>0$}\}
is a closed subgroup of
\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}GL(V_{i})
and is isomorphic to
\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}},i>0}GL(V_{i})
. Moreover the function

\Phi:G^{\iota}\rightarrow\left.\left\{(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}GL(V_{i})\ \right|g_{-i}=(g_{i}^{*})^{-1}\text{ for all $i>0$}\right\}

defined by
\Phi(g)=(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
is a well-defined isomorphism of algebraic groups.

(e)

The restriction of the adjoint action of
G
to
G^{\iota}
acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
and, under the description of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
as a subspace of
\oplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}},i\neq(2m-1)}Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})
in (b), this action is given by

Ad(g)(X)\mapsto(g_{i+2}X_{i}g_{i}^{-1})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}},i\neq(2m-1)}

for all
g\in G^{\iota}
and where
\Phi(g)=(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
) For
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
V_{i}
is the eigenspace of
\iota(t)
with eigenvalue
t^{i}
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
. Moreover when
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\neq j
, these eigenvalues
t^{i}
and
t^{j}
are distinct. Because
g\iota(t)=\iota(t)g
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
when
g\in G^{\iota}
, then
g
preserves these eigenspaces and we get
g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
g_{i}\in GL(V_{i})
follows easily because
g\in GL(V)
and
g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
.

Because we have
\langle g(v),g(v^{\prime})\rangle=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and our conditions on the direct sum decomposition, we get for all
v\in V_{i}
and
v^{\prime}\in V_{-i}
that

\langle g_{i}(v),g_{-i}(v^{\prime})\rangle=\langle g_{-i}^{*}g_{i}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle\quad\Rightarrow\quad(g_{-i}^{*})g_{i}=Id_{V_{i}}

for all
i\in I
.

\Leftarrow
) If
g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
,
g_{i}\in GL(V_{i})
and
(g_{-i})^{*}g_{i}=Id_{V_{i}}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, then
g
is an automorphism and, because of the condition on our direct sum decomposition of
V
and the fact that
(g_{-i})^{*}g_{i}=Id_{V_{i}}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we get that
g
preserves the non-degenerate form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
, in other words
g\in G
. Because
g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
, we get that
g
commutes with
\iota(t)
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
and
g\in G^{\iota}
. (a) is proved.

(b)
\Rightarrow
) Because
Ad(\iota(t))X=t^{2}X
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
when
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, we have
\iota(t)X=t^{2}X\iota(t)
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
. So if
v\in V_{i}
, then
\iota(t)X(v)=t^{2}X\iota(t)(v)=t^{i+2}X(v)
and consequently
X(V_{i})\in V_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq(2m-1)
. Note that this last equation means also that
X(V_{2m-1})=0

Note that if
i\geq 1
, then
-i=i-2
if and only if
i=1
. Because
\langle X(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,X(v^{\prime})\rangle=0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and our conditions on the direct sum decomposition, we get for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 1
and for all
v\in V_{-i}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{i-2}
that

\langle X_{-i}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,X_{i-2}(v^{\prime})\rangle=0.

In this last equation, if we substitute

X_{-i}(u_{-i,s})=\sum_{q^{\prime}=1}^{{\delta}_{i-2}}\xi_{q^{\prime},s}^{(-i)}u_{(-i+2),q^{\prime}}\quad\text{ and }\quad X_{(i-2)}(u_{(i-2),r})=\sum_{q=1}^{{\delta}_{i}}\xi_{q,r}^{(i-2)}u_{i,q},

we get for
i\geq 3
that
\epsilon\xi_{r,s}^{(-i)}+\epsilon\xi_{s,r}^{(i-2)}=0
for all
1\leq s\leq{\delta}_{i}
and
1\leq r\leq{\delta}_{i-2}
and thus
X_{-i}=-X_{i-2}^{T}
. While if
i=1
, then we get that
\xi_{r,s}^{(-1)}+\epsilon\xi_{s,r}^{(-1)}=0
for all
1\leq r,s\leq{\delta}_{1}
and thus
X_{-1}=-\epsilon X_{-1}^{T}
.

\Leftarrow
) If
X_{-i}=-X_{i-2}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq 3
, and
X_{-1}=-\epsilon X_{-1}^{T}
, then we get that
\langle X(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,X(v^{\prime})\rangle=0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
by only checking it on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
and this means that
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. If we add the condition
X(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq(2m-1)
, we have that
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. The rest of the statements in (b) are easily proved.

(c)
\Rightarrow
) Because
Ad(\iota(t))Z=Z
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
when
Z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, we have
\iota(t)Z=Z\iota(t)
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
. So if
v\in V_{i}
, then
\iota(t)Z(v)=Z\iota(t)(v)=t^{i}Z(v)
and consequently
Z(V_{i})\in V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

Because
\langle Z(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,Z(v^{\prime})\rangle=0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and our conditions on the direct sum decomposition, we get for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and for all
v\in V_{i}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{-i}
that

\langle Z_{i}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,Z_{-i}(v^{\prime})\rangle=0.

In this last equation, if we substitute

Z_{i}(u_{i,s})=\sum_{q^{\prime}=1}^{{\delta}_{i}}\zeta_{q^{\prime},s}^{(i)}u_{i,q^{\prime}}\quad\text{ and }\quad Z_{-i}(u_{-i,r})=\sum_{q=1}^{{\delta}_{i}}\zeta_{q,r}^{(-i)}u_{-i,q},

we get for
i\geq 1
that
\zeta_{r,s}^{(i)}+\zeta_{s,r}^{(-i)}=0
for all
1\leq s,r\leq{\delta}_{i}
and for
i\leq-1
that
\epsilon\zeta_{r,s}^{(i)}+\epsilon\zeta_{s,r}^{(-i)}=0
for all
1\leq s,r\leq{\delta}_{i}
.
Thus
Z_{-i}=-Z_{i}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

\Leftarrow
) If
Z_{-i}=-Z_{i}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, then we get that
\langle Z(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,Z(v^{\prime})\rangle=0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
by only checking it on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
and this means that
Z\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. If we add the condition
Z(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have that
Z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. The rest of the statements in (d) are easily proved.

(d) follows easily from (a).

(e) follows easily from (b) and the fact that
Ad(g)(X)=gXg^{-1}
for all
g\in G^{\iota}
.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

The dimension
dim({\mathfrak{g}}_{2})
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
is equal to

\left(\frac{(\delta_{1}-\epsilon)\delta_{1}}{2}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
1\leq i< (2m-1)\end{subarray}}\delta_{i}\delta_{i+2}

Proof.

This follows easily from (b) of the previous proposition.
∎

We will now present the combinatorics needed to enumerate the
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is said to be
symmetric
if and only if
c(\tau(b))=c(b)
for all
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
. Given such a symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, we can associated to it a unique well-defined
coefficient function

{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
as follows:
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})=c(b)
where
b
is any
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b\in{\mathcal{O}}
. Reciprocally given a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, we can associated to it a unique well-defined symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{N}}
as follows:
c(b)={\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
, where
{\mathcal{O}}
is the unique
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit containing
b
.

Clearly the set of symmetric functions
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is in bijection with the set of coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
.

Given a symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and its associated coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, we can associated to them their
dimension vector

\delta({\mathbf{c}})=\delta(c)=(\delta_{i}({\mathbf{c}}))_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}=(\delta_{i}(c))_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
as follows:

\delta_{i}(c)=\delta_{i}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\end{subarray}}c(b).

It is easy to verify that
\delta_{-i}(c)=\delta_{i}(c)
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

Notation 3.5
.

Denote by
{\mathfrak{C}}
: the set of coefficient functions
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. If
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-tuple such that
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. We will denote by
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
: the set of coefficient functions
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\delta({\mathbf{c}})=\delta
.

Notation 3.6
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
be an
{\mathcal{I}}
-tuple such that
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and let
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
be a coefficient function of dimension
\delta
. Denote by
V({\mathbf{c}})
: the direct sum as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded vector space and as
\epsilon
-representations of
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of
V({\mathcal{O}})
defined in
2.15
with a
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}=\coprod_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\coprod_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})\}

such that, for
i,i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
, we have

\langle v_{i}^{j}(b),v_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $j^{\prime}=j$, $b^{\prime}=\tau(b)$, $i>0$ and $i+i^{\prime}=0$; }\\
\epsilon,&\text{if $j^{\prime}=j$, $b^{\prime}=\tau(b)$, $i<0$ and $i+i^{\prime}=0$; }\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

This is just from the formulae of
2.11
adapted to our situation. Here
0\not\in{\mathcal{I}}

We will denote by
{\mathfrak{g}}(V({\mathbf{c}}))
: the Lie algebra corresponding to
(V({\mathbf{c}}),\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}})
. In other words,
{\mathfrak{g}}(V({\mathbf{c}}))
is the vector space of linear transformations
X:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
such that
\langle X(u),v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}+\langle u,X(v)\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=0
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
.

From our construction, we have that
V({\mathbf{c}})=\oplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})
, where

V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})
is
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of
V_{i}({\mathcal{O}})
. We get that
\dim(V_{i}({\mathbf{c}}))=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. We get this last equation from

\displaystyle\dim(V_{i}({\mathbf{c}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})\dim(V_{i}({\mathcal{O}}))=\sum_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\sum_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}c(b)\dim(V_{i}(b))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\end{subarray}}c(b)=\delta_{i}({\mathbf{c}}),

because
\dim(V_{i}(b))=1
when
i\in Supp(b)
.

Let the three
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformations

E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}}),\quad H_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})\quad\text{ and }\quad F_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})

denote the direct sum as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation of
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of the linear transformations

E_{\mathcal{O}}:V({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V({\mathcal{O}}),\quad H_{\mathcal{O}}:V({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V({\mathcal{O}})\quad\text{ and }\quad F_{\mathcal{O}}:V({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V({\mathcal{O}}).

By applying proposition
2.16
on each summand, we get that these three linear transformations are such that

•

E_{\mathbf{c}}
is of degree
2
,
H_{\mathbf{c}}
is of degree
0
and
F_{\mathbf{c}}
is of degree
-2
;

•

E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}
belong to the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}(V({\mathbf{c}}))
;

•

[H_{\mathbf{c}},E_{\mathbf{c}}]=2E_{\mathbf{c}}
,
[H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}]=-2F_{\mathbf{c}}
and
[E_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}]=H_{\mathbf{c}}
; in other words, the triple
(E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}})
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded Jacobson-Morozov triple for the Lie algebra of
(V({\mathbf{c}}),\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}})
;

•

the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation
\phi_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
given by

\phi_{{\mathbf{c}},i}={E_{\mathbf{c}}}|_{V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})}:V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V_{i+2}({\mathbf{c}})\quad\text{for all $i\in{\mathcal{I}}$ such that $(i+2)\in{\mathcal{I}}$},

is such that
(V({\mathbf{c}}),\phi_{\mathbf{c}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}})
is an
\epsilon
-representation.

As such we can express these linear transformations on the elements of the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
using the expressions in
2.15
. For example, let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b
, then, if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has no overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{ if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i>0$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i<0$;}\end{cases}

while if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
b=b(i_{1},j_{1},k_{1})\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, then we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{ if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i\geq 1$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i<-1$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is below the principal diagonal;}\\
-\epsilon v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is above the principal diagonal;}\\
(-\epsilon)^{k_{1}}v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is on the principal diagonal;}\end{cases}

3.7.

Given an element
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, we will now associate to it a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
as follows. By lemma
3.2
(b) and with the notation of this lemma,
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
is equivalent to giving the
\epsilon
-representation
(V,\phi_{V},\langle\ ,\ \rangle)
,where

\phi_{V,i}=X|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}\quad v\mapsto X(v)\quad\text{ for all $v\in V_{i}$}

is the restriction of
X
on
V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(i+2)\in{\mathcal{I}}
. By the theorem of Krull-Remak-Schmidt, this
\epsilon
-representation can be written in essentially unique way as a direct sum of indecomposable
\epsilon
-representations (up to isomorphism). We saw in proposition
2.16
that the set of indecomposable
\epsilon
-representations is in bijection with the set
{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
. More precisely, for each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
, we have constructed a representative
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
of the isomorphism class of the indecomposable
\epsilon
-representation. Denote by
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}({\mathcal{O}})
: the multiplicity of the indecomposable
\epsilon
-representation
(V({\mathcal{O}}),\phi_{\mathcal{O}},\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathcal{O}})
(up to isomorphism). So the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is defined by
{\mathcal{O}}\mapsto{\mathbf{c}}_{X}({\mathcal{O}})
.

3.8.

We will now constructed an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
such that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
. We will define this isomorphism on the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
of
V({\mathbf{c}})
and
{\mathcal{B}}
of
V
.

Let
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
. We get easily that

\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid i\in Supp(b)\}\rightarrow\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-i=\sigma(i)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}\text{ defined by }b\mapsto\tau(b)

is a bijection. Let
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
be the unique symmetric function corresponding to the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. Fix a total order
b_{1}>b_{2}>\dots>b_{n}
on the set
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid i\in Supp(b)\}
such that
c(b_{r})\geq c(b_{s})
whenever
r<s
for
1\leq r,s\leq n
and let
b^{\prime}_{1}>b^{\prime}_{2}>\dots>b^{\prime}_{n}
be the total order on the set
\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-i=\sigma(i)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}
such that
b^{\prime}_{r}=\tau(b_{r})
for
1\leq r\leq n
.
Consider the Young diagram corresponding to the partition
c(b_{1})\geq c(b_{2})\geq\dots\geq c(b_{n})
. So the row indexed by
b_{r}
will have
c(b_{r})
columns. We would get the same Young diagram
c(b^{\prime}_{1})\geq c(b^{\prime}_{2})\geq\dots\geq c(b^{\prime}_{n})
if we had started with the total order
b^{\prime}_{1}>b^{\prime}_{2}>\dots>b^{\prime}_{n}
on the set
\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-i=\sigma(i)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}
. This follows from the fact that
c
is symmetric. This partition is a partition of the integer
\delta_{i}
from
3.6
. In this Young diagram, we fill the boxes with the integers from 1 to
\delta_{i}
in strictly increasing order from left to right and top to bottom. We define
T_{{\mathbf{c}},i}:V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V_{i}
(respectively
T_{{\mathbf{c}},-i}:V_{-i}({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V_{-i}
) to be the unique linear transformation such that if the entry in the box at row
r
and column
s
is
j
, then

T_{{\mathbf{c}},i}(v_{i}^{s}(b_{r}))=u_{i,j}
(respectively
T_{{\mathbf{c}},-i}(v_{-i}^{s}(b^{\prime}_{r}))=T_{{\mathbf{c}},-i}(v_{-i}^{s}(\tau(b_{r})))=u_{-i,j}
).

Finally
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V_{\mathbf{c}}\rightarrow V
is the direct sum of the linear transformations
T_{{\mathbf{c}},i}:V_{i}({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V_{i}
and
T_{{\mathbf{c}},-i}:V_{-i}({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V_{-i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
. Note that the dimension vector
\delta({\mathbf{c}})
of
{\mathbf{c}}
is
\delta({\mathbf{c}})=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
, where
\delta_{i}=\dim(V_{i})
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

With the above definition, we have that
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism such that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
.

Proof.

From our construction, it is clear that
T_{\mathbf{c}}
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism. We use the fact that
T_{\mathbf{c}}
restricted to
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is a bijection with the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
of
V
. To check that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
, it is enough to check it on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. We want to consider
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b)),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))\rangle
where
i,i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\in Supp(b)
,
1\leq s\leq c(b)
,
i^{\prime}\in Supp(b^{\prime})
and
1\leq s^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
. Because
T_{\mathbf{c}}
is
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded, our hypothesis in
3.1
on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}
of
V
and our construction in
3.6
, we get if
i+i^{\prime}\neq 0
, that

\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b)),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))\rangle=0=\langle v_{i}^{s}(b),v_{i^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}.

Now let
i^{\prime}=-i
and assume first that
i>0
. Consider the same total orders on the sets
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid i\in Supp(b)\}
and
\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-i=\sigma(i)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}
as in
3.8
. Proceeding as above with the Young diagram, assume that
b=b_{r}
in the total order
b_{1}>b_{2}>\dots>b_{n}
on the set
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid i\in Supp(b)\}
and
b^{\prime}=b^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}=\tau(b_{r^{\prime}})
in the total order
b^{\prime}_{1}>b^{\prime}_{2}>\dots>b^{\prime}_{n}
on the set
\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-i=\sigma(b^{\prime})\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}
, the entry in the box at row
r
and column
s
is
j
and the entry in the box at row
r^{\prime}
and column
s^{\prime}
is
j^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b)),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b_{r})),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}))\rangle=\langle u_{i,j},u_{-i,j^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $j=j^{\prime}$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $r=r^{\prime}$ and $s=s^{\prime}$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If we now consider the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
in
V({\mathbf{c}})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}^{s}(b),v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{i}^{s}(b_{r}),v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\langle v_{i}^{s}(b_{r}),v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(\tau(b_{r^{\prime}}))\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $r=r^{\prime}$ and $s=s^{\prime}$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

So if
i>0
, then
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b)),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))\rangle=\langle v_{i}^{s}(b),v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
.

If
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i<0
, then

\displaystyle\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b)),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\epsilon\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b))\rangle

\displaystyle=\epsilon\langle v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}),v_{i}^{s}(b)\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\langle v_{i}^{s}(b),v_{-i}^{s^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}.

We have proved that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle
for all
u,v\in V
.

∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
be as in
3.1
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}
denotes the set of
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. Then the map

\Upsilon:{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}\quad\text{ defined by }\quad G^{\iota}\cdot X\mapsto{\mathbf{c}}_{X},

is a well-defined bijection.

Proof.

By our last observation in
3.7
, if
G^{\iota}\cdot X=G^{\iota}\cdot X^{\prime}
, then the corresponding
\epsilon
-representations for
X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic, we get that
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}={\mathbf{c}}_{X^{\prime}}
. So the function
\Upsilon
is well-defined.

To show that
\Upsilon
is an injective function, assume that
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}={\mathbf{c}}_{X^{\prime}}
, then this means that, by the Krull-Remak-Schmidt theorem, the
\epsilon
-representations for
X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic. Note that in the case of the orthogonal representation (i.e.
\epsilon=1
), the group
G
is not
SO(V)
, but rather the group
{\widehat{G}}=O(V)
, but it is easy to see that
G^{\iota}={\widehat{G}}^{\iota}
as seen in lemma
3.2
and the isomorphism is the same for the case of
SO(V)
and
O(V)
. So
X
and
X^{\prime}
are in the same
G^{\iota}
-orbit and
G^{\iota}\cdot X=G^{\iota}\cdot X^{\prime}

To prove that
\Upsilon
is surjective, we need to show that there is an element
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
such that
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}={\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Consider
X=T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T_{\mathbf{c}}^{-1}:V\rightarrow V
, where
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
is the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism defined in
3.8
. Because
E_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}(V({\mathbf{c}}))
and lemma
3.9
,
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. From our construction of
E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
, it is isomorphic as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded and
\epsilon
-representation to the direct sum of
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of the linear transformation
E_{\mathcal{O}}:V({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow V({\mathcal{O}})
. By carrying this direct sum using
T_{\mathbf{c}}
to
V
, we do get that
{\mathbf{c}}_{X}={\mathbf{c}}
and the proof is complete.
∎

Notation 3.11
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
be such that
\delta_{i}=\dim(V_{i})
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
as in
3.1
. If
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
, we will denote by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
: the unique
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
such that
\Upsilon({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})={\mathbf{c}}
.

Remark 3.12
.

Using the isomorphism
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
, we can transfer results about the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
in the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
to the orbit of
E_{\mathbf{c}}
in the Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}(V({\mathbf{c}}))
.

3.13.

To compute the dimension of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
corresponding to the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
, we need to recall Lusztig’s construction of a parabolic subalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}_{X}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
associated to an element
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
and the formula for the dimension of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
involving the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{p}}_{X}
, where
X\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. It is known that we can find a Lie algebra homomorphism
\phi:{\mathfrak{s}l}_{2}({\mathbf{k}})\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}
such that

\phi\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}=X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2},\qquad\phi\begin{pmatrix}1&\phantom{-}0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},\qquad\phi\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}.

Denote by
\widetilde{\phi}:SL_{2}({\mathbf{k}})\rightarrow G
: the homomorphism of algebraic groups whose differential is
\phi
and by
\iota^{\prime}:{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}\rightarrow G
: the homomorphism defined by

\iota^{\prime}(t)=\widetilde{\phi}\begin{pmatrix}t&0\phantom{-}\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{ for all $t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}$}.

This gives us a second grading for the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}=\oplus_{r}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}})
where

({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}})=\{X\in{\mathfrak{g}}\mid Ad(\iota^{\prime}(t))(X)=t^{r}X\quad\text{ for all $t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}$}\}

and, because
\iota^{\prime}({\mathbf{k}}^{\times})\subset G^{\iota}
, we have the direct sum decomposition

{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigoplus_{r,r^{\prime}}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}}_{r^{\prime}}).

In 5.2 of
[
9
]
, Lusztig defines the
parabolic subalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}_{X}
as

{\mathfrak{p}}_{X}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,r^{\prime}\\
r^{\prime}\leq r\end{subarray}}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}}_{r^{\prime}})

Proposition 3.15

(Lusztig)
.

If
X\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
, then

\dim({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})=\dim({\mathfrak{g}}_{0})-\dim({\mathfrak{p}}_{0})+\dim({\mathfrak{p}}_{2}),

where
{\mathfrak{p}}
is the parabolic subalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}_{X}
associated above to
X
.

Proof.

See 4.1 in
[
10
]
.
∎

We now want to use this formula to compute the dimension
\dim({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})
of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}
in term of the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. This is done in the next proposition.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
and consider the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
corresponding to
{\mathbf{c}}
. Then the dimension
\dim({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})
of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is equal to

\left[\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i>0\end{subarray}}\delta_{i}^{2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i>0\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
0<i<(2m-1)\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\\
(i+2)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})\\
\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
\tau(b)\neq b^{\prime}\\
1\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b^{\prime})\leq\lambda(b)\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b)\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b)(c(b)-1)\\
\\
&+\frac{(1-\epsilon)}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b)\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b)\end{aligned}\right]

Recall that the value
\lambda(b)
for
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
has been defined in notation
2.18
.

Proof.

Taking into account our remark
3.12
, we will assume that
V=V({\mathbf{c}})
. We have that
\dim(V_{i})=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
from the fact that
\delta({\mathbf{c}})=\delta
.
We have constructed in
3.6
a Jacobson-Morozov triple
(E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}})
with
E_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
; in other words, we have

[H_{\mathbf{c}},E_{\mathbf{c}}]=2E_{\mathbf{c}},\qquad[H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}]=-2F_{\mathbf{c}},\qquad[E_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}]=H_{\mathbf{c}},

where
E_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
,
H_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and
F_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}
, and such that
E_{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Thus we have a Lie algebra homomorphism
\phi:{\mathfrak{s}l}_{2}({\mathbf{k}})\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}
such that

\phi\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}=E_{\mathbf{c}},\qquad\phi\begin{pmatrix}1&\phantom{-}0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}=H_{\mathbf{c}},\qquad\phi\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=F_{\mathbf{c}}.

As in definition
3.14
, let
\widetilde{\phi}:SL_{2}({\mathbf{k}})\rightarrow G
be the homomorphism of algebraic groups whose differential is
\phi
and by
\iota^{\prime}:{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}\rightarrow G
: the homomorphism defined by

\iota^{\prime}(t)=\widetilde{\phi}\begin{pmatrix}t&0\phantom{-}\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{ for all $t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}$.}

We have the grading for the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}=\oplus_{r}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}})
where

({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}})=\{X\in{\mathfrak{g}}\mid Ad(\iota^{\prime}(t))(X)=t^{r}X\quad\text{ for all $t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}$}\}

By taking the derivative, we get that

({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}})=\{X\in{\mathfrak{g}}\mid ad(H_{\mathbf{c}})(X)=rX\}.

By lemma
3.2
(c), we have that

\dim({\mathfrak{g}}_{0})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i>0\end{subarray}}\delta_{i}^{2}.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be the parabolic subalgebra associated to the nilpotent element
E_{\mathbf{c}}
as in the definition
3.14
. From the definition of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, we get that

{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}=\bigoplus_{r\geq 0}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}=\bigoplus_{r\geq 2}({}_{r}{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}).

By our description of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
in lemma
3.2
, we get that

{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}=\bigoplus_{r\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
0<i\end{subarray}}\{(Z_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i})\in Hom(V_{i},V_{i})\mid ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})Z_{i}=rZ_{i}\}

and
{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}={\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{2}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2}
where

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{2}=\bigoplus_{r\geq 2}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
0<i<(2m-1)\end{subarray}}\{(X_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2})\in Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})\mid ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})X_{i}=rX_{i}\}

and

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2}=\bigoplus_{r\geq 2}\left\{(X_{-1}:V_{-1}\rightarrow V_{1})\in Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})\left|\begin{aligned} &X_{-1}=-\epsilon X_{-1}^{*}\text{ and }\\
&ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})X_{-1}=rX_{-1}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}

We can give bases for the spaces
Hom(V_{i},V_{i})
and
Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})
that are eigenvectors for
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})
. From our construction of the Jacobson-Morozov triple
\{E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}}\}
and notation
3.6
, we have for each
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
a basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\in V_{i}\mid b\in{\mathbf{B}},i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}

of
V_{i}
such that
H_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=h_{i}(b)v_{i}^{j}(b)
for all
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
i\in Supp(b)
and
1\leq j\leq c(b)
. For each
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
, let
Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\in Hom(V_{i},V_{i})
be defined on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
by

Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime}))=\begin{cases}v_{i}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}),&\text{if $b^{\prime\prime}=b$ and $j^{\prime\prime}=j$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Clearly these linear transformations
Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
with
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
form a basis of
Hom(V_{i},V_{i})
and
Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
is an eigenvector of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})
with

ad(H_{\mathbf{c}})(Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})})=(h_{i}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}

by computing both sides on the basis vector
v_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
. Consequently

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}_{0})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i>0\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})\\
(h_{i}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i>0\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})

because
(h_{i}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))\geq 0
is equivalent to
(i-h_{i}(b))-(i-h_{i}(b^{\prime}))\geq 0
. This is simply
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})
.

Let now
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
0< i<(2m-1)
. For each
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
i\in Supp(b)
,
(i+2)\in Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
, let
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\in Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})
be defined on the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
by

X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime}))=\begin{cases}v_{(i+2)}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}),&\text{if $b^{\prime\prime}=b$ and $j^{\prime\prime}=j$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Clearly these linear transformations
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
with
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
i\in Supp(b)
,
(i+2)\in Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
form a basis of
Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})
and
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
is an eigenvector of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})
with

ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}})(X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})})=(h_{(i+2)}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}

by computing both sides on the basis vector
v_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
. Consequently

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
0< i< (2m-1)\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\\
(i+2)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
(h_{(i+2)}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))\geq 2\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
0< i< (2m-1)\\
(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\\
(i+2)\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})

because
(h_{(i+2)}(b^{\prime})-h_{i}(b))\geq 2
is equivalent to
(i-h_{i}(b))-((i+2)-h_{(i+2)}(b^{\prime}))\geq 0
. This is simply
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})
.

Finally we need to compute the dimension of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. If we use the basis as in notation
3.6
appearing in
V_{-1}
, then we get the basis

\{v_{-1}^{j}(b)\mid b\in{\mathbf{B}},-1\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}.

of
V_{-1}
.

The involution
\tau
restricted to
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-1\in Supp(b)\}
gives a bijection between
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid-1\in Supp(b)\}
and
\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid 1\in Supp(b)\}
. We can use this bijection to index the basis used in notation
3.6
for
V_{1}
and also the fact that the function
c
is symmetric. Thus we get the basis

\{v_{1}^{j}(\tau(b))\mid b\in{\mathbf{B}},-1\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}.

of
V_{1}
.

For these bases, we have

\langle v_{1}^{j^{\prime}}(\tau(b^{\prime})),v_{-1}^{j}(b)\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\epsilon\langle v_{-1}^{j}(b),v_{1}^{j^{\prime}}(\tau(b^{\prime}))\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{$b=b^{\prime},j^{\prime}=j$;}\\
0,&\text{ otherwise;}\end{cases}

where
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
.

For each homomorphism
X_{-1}:V_{-1}\rightarrow V_{1}
, we can write

X_{-1}(v_{-1}^{j}(b))=\sum_{(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})}\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})}v_{1}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(\tau(b^{\prime\prime}))

and

X_{-1}(v_{-1}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}))=\sum_{(b^{\prime\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime\prime})}\xi_{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}^{(b^{\prime\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime\prime})}v_{1}^{j^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\tau(b^{\prime\prime\prime}))

where the sums run over the set of pairs
(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})
,
(b^{\prime\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime\prime})
such that
b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b^{\prime\prime})\cap Supp(b^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
1\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime\prime})
and
1\leq j^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime\prime\prime})
.

If we add the condition of being in the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, that is

\langle X_{-1}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,X_{-1}(v^{\prime})\rangle=0\quad\text{ for all $v,v^{\prime}\in V_{-1}$,}

this means that when
v=v_{-1}^{j}(b)
and
v^{\prime}=v_{-1}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})
, we get

(1)

\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}+\epsilon\xi_{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}^{(b,j)}=0

for all
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
. The converse is also true. More precisely, if we impose the conditions above for all these coefficients
\xi
, then the corresponding linear transformation
X_{-1}
belongs to the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
.

We will use this to give a basis of the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. For
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq c(b^{\prime})
. First we assume that
(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})\neq(b,j)
, then we define the linear transformation
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}:V_{-1}\rightarrow V_{1}
as follows:

X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v_{-1}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime}))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}v_{1}^{j^{\prime}}(\tau(b^{\prime})),&\text{ if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b,j)$};\\
\\
-\epsilon v_{1}^{j}(\tau(b)),&\text{ if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})$;}\\
\\
0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In other words, we have

\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

\xi_{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}^{(b^{\prime\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime\prime})}=\begin{cases}-\epsilon,&\text{if $(b^{\prime\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime\prime})=(b,j)$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and equation (1) is satisfied. Thus
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
belongs to the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. By computing on all of the basis vector
v_{-1}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
, we get that

X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=-\epsilon X_{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}^{(b,j)}.

X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
is an eigenvector of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})
with

ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=(h_{1}(\tau(b))-h_{-1}(b^{\prime}))X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}

by computing both sides on the basis vector
v_{-1}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
. Note that we have used here lemma
2.14
(a).

Note that
(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})\neq(b,j)
is equivalent to either
b^{\prime}\neq b
or (
b^{\prime}=b
and
j^{\prime}\neq j
).

If we are in the orthogonal case, in other words when
\epsilon=1
, then we get from equation (1) that
\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}=0
for all
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b)
and
1\leq j\leq c(b)
. We can totally order the finite set
\{(b,j)\mid b\in{\mathbf{B}},-1\in Supp(b)\}
. We will write
<
for this total order. With this, we get easily that the linear transformations
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
where
(b,j)<(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is a basis of the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
and, because of our computation of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})
on these basis elements, we get that

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
b^{\prime}\neq b\\
-1\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})\\
(h_{1}(\tau(b))-h_{-1}(b^{\prime})\geq 2\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
-h_{-1}(b)\geq 1\end{subarray}}c(b)(c(b)-1)

Note that
h_{1}(\tau(b))-h_{-1}(b^{\prime})\geq 2
is equivalent to
-1-h_{-1}(b)\geq 1-h_{1}(\tau(b^{\prime}))
by lemma
2.14
(a). This is simply
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(\tau(b^{\prime}))
. Similarly
-h_{-1}(b)\geq 1
is equivalent to
-1-h_{-1}(b)\geq 0
. This is
\lambda(b)\geq 0
. In the first sum, we can use
\tau(b^{\prime})
rather than
b^{\prime}
. Finally we get that

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
\tau(b^{\prime})\neq b\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
1\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b)\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b)(c(b)-1)

If we are in the symplectic case, in other words when
\epsilon=-1
, then we don’t necessarily have that
\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}=0
. For
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
,
-1\in Supp(b)
and
1\leq j\leq c(b)
. Then we can define the linear transformation
X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}:V_{-1}\rightarrow V_{1}
as follows:

X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}(v_{-1}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime}))=\begin{cases}v_{1}^{j}(\tau(b)),&\text{ if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b,j)$};\\
\\
0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In other words, we have

\xi_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b,j)$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

and equation (1) is satisfied. Consequently
X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}
belongs to the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
.

X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}
is an eigenvector of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})
with

ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}=(h_{1}(\tau(b))-h_{-1}(b))X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}.

As above we can totally order the finite set
\{(b,j)\mid b\in{\mathbf{B}},-1\in Supp(b)\}
. We will write
<
for this total order. With this, we get easily that the linear transformations
X_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
where
(b,j)<(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})
with the linear transformations
X_{(b,j)}^{(b,j)}
form a basis of the
Hom(V_{-1},V_{1})
-component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
and, because of our computation of
ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{-1}})
on these basis elements, we get that

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
\tau(b^{\prime})\neq b\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
1\in Supp(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b)\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b)(c(b)-1)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
-1\in Supp(b)\\
h_{\beta}(1)\geq 1\end{subarray}}c(b)

For the first two sums above, it is similar to the orthogonal case. For the third sum above, we have to noted that
h_{1}(\tau(b))-h_{-1}(b)\geq 2
is equivalent to
-1-h_{-1}(b)-1-h_{-1}(b)\geq 0
. This is simply
\lambda(b)\geq 0
.

From all the previous computation, the proposition follows.
∎

We will now describe the partition of
\dim(V)
corresponding to the Jordan type for any element in an orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
X
be an element of the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
and let
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
the symmetric function corresponding to
{\mathbf{c}}
. Then the partition corresponding to the Jordan type of
X
is

\prod_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}|Supp(b)|^{c(b)}.

Here we have described the partition in multiplicative form.

Proof.

Taking into account our remark
3.12
, we can assume that
V=V({\mathbf{c}})
. If
(E_{\bf c},H_{\bf c},F_{\bf c})
is the Jacobson-Morozov triple corresponding to the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
as in
3.6
, then we can take
E_{\bf c}
as a representative for the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. As a representation of the quiver
A_{2m}
, we know that
E_{\bf c}
is a direct sum over
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
of
c(b)
copies of an indecomposable representation
V(b)
as in notation
2.11
and with the formulae in
2.15
. Thus from these, we get that there is a unique Jordan bloc for the restriction of
E_{\mathbf{c}}
on
V(b)
. In other words,
V(b)
is a cyclic subspace and the partition of the restriction of
E_{\mathbf{c}}
on
V(b)
is
|Supp(b)|
. From this, we get that the Jordan type of
E_{\mathbf{c}}
is as stated in the proposition.
∎

3.18.

We want to give now a compact way to describe the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
corresponding to the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
.

Definition 3.19
.

A
symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
is the
{\mathcal{I}}
-diagram with its boxes filled with elements of
{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
c_{i,j}
denoted the entry in the row indexed by
i
and in the column indexed by
j
, then we have
c_{-j,-i}=c_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq j
. An
orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
is a symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-Young tableau with the added condition that
c_{i,-i}
is an even integer for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
(i.e. the entries on the principal diagonal are even).

Example 3.20
.

For
m=3
and
{\mathcal{I}}=\{5,3,1,-1,-3,-5\}
, then

{\mathcal{T}}
=

-5

-3

-1

1

3

5

{\phantom{-}}5

1

{\phantom{-}}3

1

{\phantom{-}}1

0

-1

0

-3

1

-5

1

is a symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau, but not an orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau.

{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
=

-5

-3

-1

1

3

5

{\phantom{-}}5

2

{\phantom{-}}3

1

{\phantom{-}}1

1

-1

1

-3

1

-5

2

is an orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau. We have kept the indices for the rows and columns inscribed.

Definition 3.21
.

Given the symplectic (respectively orthogonal ) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}=(c_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}
, we define its
dimension vector

\delta({\mathcal{T}})
as the
{\mathcal{I}}
-tuple
\delta({\mathcal{T}})=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, where

\delta_{i}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}\quad\text{for all $i\in{\mathcal{I}}$.}

Example 3.22
.

If
{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
are the symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux of example
3.20
, then their dimension vectors are respectively

\displaystyle\delta({\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{5},\delta_{3},\delta_{1},\delta_{-1},\delta_{-3},\delta_{-5})=(3,10,17,17,10,3)\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\delta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{5},\delta_{3},\delta_{1},\delta_{-1},\delta_{-3},\delta_{-5})=(7,13,14,14,13,7).

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
: the set of symplectic (respectively orthogonal ) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{T}}
of dimension
\delta({\mathcal{T}})=\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
and by
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\delta}
: the set of symplectic (respectively orthogonal ) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{R}}=(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\geq j}
such that

r_{i,i}=\delta_{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad(r_{i,j}-r_{i+2,j}-r_{i,j-2}+r_{i+2,j-2})\geq 0\quad\text{ for all $i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}$ and $i\geq j$}

where we set
r_{i,j}=0
if either
i\not\in{\mathcal{I}}
or
j\not\in{\mathcal{I}}
or both. Then the map
\varTheta:{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{R}}_{\delta}

\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})=\varTheta((c_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j})=(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\geq j}\quad\text{where $r_{i,j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq j\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}$}

for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
. is a well-defined bijection.

Proof.

This is a known result. See for example section 2 in
[
1
]
. In fact in
[
1
]
, the
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux are not necessarily symmetric, but it is easy to check that
\varTheta
sends symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux to symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux.

In the orthogonal case, we also have to see that the entries
r_{i,-i}
are even for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
. But this follows from the fact that

\displaystyle r_{i,-i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\end{subarray}}c_{u,-u}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\\
(u+v)>0\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\\
0>(u+v)\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\end{subarray}}c_{u,-u}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\\
(u+v)>0\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\\
0>(u+v)\end{subarray}}c_{-v,-u}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\end{subarray}}c_{u,-u}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-i\geq v\\
(u+v)>0\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}

and the fact that the
c_{u,-u}
are even.

The inverse map of
\varTheta
is
\varTheta^{-1}:{\mathfrak{R}}_{\delta}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
is

\varTheta^{-1}((r_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j})=(c_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\geq j}\quad\text{where $c_{i,j}=(r_{i,j}-r_{i+2,j}-r_{i,j-2}+r_{i+2,j-2})$}

We have also to prove that the entries
c_{i,-i}
are even for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
in the orthogonal case. This follows from

c_{i,-i}=r_{i,-i}-r_{i+2,-i}-r_{i,-i-2}+r_{i+2,-i-2}=r_{i,-i}-2r_{i+2,-i}+r_{i+2,-i-2}

and the fact that both
r_{i,-i}
and
r_{(i+2),-(i+2)}
are even.
∎

Example 3.24
.

If
{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
are the symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux of example
3.20
, then

\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})
=

-5

-3

-1

1

3

5

{\phantom{-}}5

3

{\phantom{-}}3

10

{\phantom{-}}1

17

17

3

and

\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})
=

-5

-1

1

3

5

{\phantom{-}}5

7

{\phantom{-}}3

13

{\phantom{-}}1

.

We have kept the indices for the rows and columns inscribed.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. To
{\mathbf{c}}
, denote the corresponding symmetric function by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. We define the symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
as follows: if
c_{i,j}
is the entry at the row
i
and column
j
with
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq j
, then

c_{i,j}=\begin{cases}c(b(i,j,0)),&\text{ if $i+j\neq 0$;}\\
c(b(i,j,0)),&\text{ if $i+j=0$ in the symplectic case;}\\
c(b(i,j,0))+c(b(i,j,1)),&\text{ if $i+j=0$ in the orthogonal case.}\end{cases}

Because
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is symmetric,
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is clearly a symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau. In the orthogonal case, we have that
c(b(i,j,0))=c(b(i,j,1))
when
i+j=0
and consequently
c_{i,-i}
is even. Thus
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is an orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\\
u+v\neq 0\end{subarray}}c(b(u,v,0))

\displaystyle+\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-u\end{subarray}}c(b(u,-u,0)),&\text{in the symplectic case;}\\
\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-u\end{subarray}}c(b(u,-u,0))+c(b(u,-u,1)),&\text{in the orthogonal case;}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\end{subarray}}c(b)=\delta_{i}.

Consequently
\delta({\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}}))=\delta
and
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}
denotes the set of
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. Then

(a)

the map
{\mathcal{T}}:{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
, where
{\mathbf{c}}\mapsto{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is a bijection;

(b)

the map
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}\quad\text{ defined by }\quad G^{\iota}\cdot X\mapsto{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}}_{X})
is a well-defined bijection.

Proof.

(a) is obvious from the definition of
{\mathcal{T}}
above and (b) is a corollary of proposition
3.10
.
∎

Notation 3.27
.

We will denote the
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
corresponding to the
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
as defined in the bijection of lemma
3.26
(b) by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}

In
[
2
]
, M. Boos and G. Cerulli Irelli, were able to describe the partial order on the
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
for symmetric quivers in the orthogonal and symplectic cases for symmetric quivers of type
A_{m}^{even}
and
A_{m}^{odd}
. We will now describe their result in our case (i.e.
|{\mathcal{I}}|
even).

Proposition 3.28

(Boos and Cerulli Irelli)
.

(In the situation of
3.1
) Let
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{T}}
,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
and the corresponding
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. Then the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is contained in the Zariski closure of the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
if and only if

(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}={\mathcal{R}}=\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})\leq\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}=(r^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}

where the inequality here between the tableaux
{\mathcal{R}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
means
r_{i,j}\leq r^{\prime}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
.

Proof.

First we need to observe that if we consider an element
X
in the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, where
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
, and, as in part (b) of lemma
3.2
, we write the linear transformations
X_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}
for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq(2m-1)
associated to
X
and that gives us a representation of the quiver
A_{2m}
, then the rank of the composition

X_{i-2}\circ X_{i-4}\circ\dots\circ X_{j+2}\circ X_{j}:V_{j}\longrightarrow V_{i}\quad\text{for $i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}$, $i>j$}

is
{r}_{i,j}^{\prime\prime}
, where
(r_{i,j}^{\prime\prime})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime\prime}=\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime})
. This is proved in section 2 in
[
1
]
for example. Note also that
{r}_{i,i}^{\prime\prime}=\dim(V_{i})
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

If we apply this observation for the two orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
, where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is contained in the Zariski closure
\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}}
of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
, then by proposition 2.8 in
[
1
]
, it follows that

(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}={\mathcal{R}}=\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})\leq\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}=(r^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}.

Proposition 2.8 in
[
1
]
states that these ranks cannot increase in a degeneration.

As for the reciprocal, we must use theorem 7.1 in
[
2
]
. In our case, this theorem means that the partial order of symplectic (respectively orthogonal) representations is the same as the partial order induced by forgetting the symplectic (respectively special orthogonal) condition and just looking at the representation of the quiver
A_{2m}
. But for the equioriented quiver of type
A_{2m}
, from theorem 5.2 in
[
1
]
, we get that

{\mathcal{R}}=\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})\leq\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}}.

Note that in the article
[
2
]
of M. Boos and G. Cerulli Irelli for orthogonal representations, the isomorphism is the orthogonal isomorphism that is used in the article, but the orthogonal isomorphism is equivalent to special orthogonal isomorphism in the case
A_{m}^{even}
as we saw in the proof of proposition
3.10
. For this reason, we can use theorem 7.1 of
[
2
]
.
∎

4.
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd.

4.1.

In this section, we will restrict ourself to the symplectic and orthogonal cases when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd. It is simpler and more similar to what we did in the previous section. The special orthogonal case will be treated in the next section. The reason for this is that special orthogonal isomorphism is more restricted that orthogonal isomorphism as we saw in
2.22
.

4.2.

In this section,

•

m
is an integer
>0
;

•

G
is the group of automorphisms of a finite dimensional vector space
V
over
{\mathbf{k}}
preserving a fixed non-degenerate symmetric (respectively skew-symmetric) form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:V\times V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
, in other words
G=O(V)
(respectively
G=Sp(V)
);

•

\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
, more precisely
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if
X:V\rightarrow V
is an endomorphism of
V
such that
\langle X(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle+\langle v_{1},X(v_{2}) \rangle=0
for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
;

•

V
is identified to its dual
V^{*}
by the isomorphism
\phi:V\rightarrow V^{*}
defined by
v\mapsto\phi_{v}
, where
\phi_{v}:V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
is
\phi_{v}(x)=\langle v,x\rangle
for all
x\in V
;

•

{\mathcal{I}}=\{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mid n\equiv 0\pmod{2},-2m<n<2m\}
;

•

\oplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}V_{i}
is a direct sum decomposition of
V
by vector subspaces
V_{i}\neq 0
such that

–

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0
whenever
v\in V_{i}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{j}
and
i+j\neq 0
;

–

V_{-i}=V_{i}^{*}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
under the identification given by
\phi
above.

Note that the restriction of the non-degenerate symmetric (respectively skew-symmetric) form bilinear
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
to
V_{0}\times V_{0}
is a non-degenerate symmetric (respectively skew-symmetric) form.

•

{\mathcal{B}}=\coprod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
is a basis of
V
such that each
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\{u_{i,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
is a basis of
V_{i}
for each
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\neq 0
, where
\delta_{i}
is the dimension of
V_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=\{u_{0,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{0}\}
(respectively
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=\{u_{0,j},u_{0,-j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}\}
) is a basis of
V_{0}
, where
\delta_{0}
(respectively
\delta_{0}=2\delta^{\prime}_{0}
) is the dimension of
V_{0}
and these bases are such that, for
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq 0
,
j\neq 0
,
1\leq r\leq\delta_{i}
and
1\leq s\leq\delta_{j}
, we have

\langle u_{i,r},u_{j,s}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $i+j=0$, $r=s$ and $i>0$;}\\
\epsilon,&\text{if $i+j=0$, $r=s$ and $i<0$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

while for
1\leq r,s\leq\delta_{0}
, we have

\langle u_{0,r},u_{0,s}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $r+s=\delta_{0}+1$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

(respectively for
1\leq|r|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}
and
1\leq|s|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}
, we have

\langle u_{0,r},u_{0,s}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $r+s=0$ and $r>0$;}\\
\epsilon,&\text{if $r+s=0$ and $r<0$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;)}\end{cases}

and, if
i\neq 0
,
1\leq s\leq\delta_{i}
,
1\leq r\leq\delta_{0}
(respectively
1\leq|r|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}
), we have

\langle u_{0,r},u_{i,s}\rangle=\langle u_{i,s},u_{0,r}\rangle=0.

•

\iota:{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}\rightarrow G
is the homomorphism defined by
\iota(t)v=t^{i}v
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
v\in V_{i}
and
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
;

•

{\mathbf{B}}
is the set of
{\mathcal{I}}
-boxes;

•

{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
denote the set of
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
in
{\mathbf{B}}
;

•

\nu=|{\mathbf{B}}/\tau|=m^{2}
;

•

In the symplectic case,
V_{0}^{+}
denotes the subspace of
V_{0}
generated by
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{+}=\{u_{0,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}\}
and
V_{0}^{-}
denotes the subspace of
V_{0}
generated by
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{-}=\{u_{0,-j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0}\}
. Obviously
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{-}
are respectively bases of
V_{0}^{+}
and
V_{0}^{-}

Lemma 4.3
.

(a)

g\in G^{\iota}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified:

•

g(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

the restriction
g_{|_{V_{i}}}=g_{i}
of
g
to
V_{i}
belongs to
GL(V_{i})
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

(g_{-i})^{*}g_{i}=Id_{V_{i}}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq 0
;

•

g_{0}\in O(V_{0})
(respectively
g_{0}\in Sp(V_{0})
) relative to the non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
restricted to
V_{0}\times V_{0}
.

Here
g_{-i}^{*}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
is defined using the identification of
V_{i}
with
V_{-i}^{*}
given by
\phi
. So
\langle g_{-i}^{*}(u),v\rangle=\langle u,g_{-i}(v)\rangle
for all
u\in V_{i}
and
v\in V_{-i}
.

(b)

X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified:

•

X(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq(2m-2)
;

•

X_{-i}=-X_{i-2}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq 4
;

•

X_{-2}=-JX^{T}_{0}
where
J
is the
\delta_{0}\times\delta_{0}
-matrix equal to

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\dots&0&1\\
0&0&\dots&1&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\udots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&1&\dots&0&0\\
1&0&\dots&0&0\end{pmatrix};

\left(\text{respectively }X_{0}=(X_{0}^{+},X_{0}^{-})\text{ and }X_{-2}=\begin{pmatrix}X_{-2}^{+}\\
X_{-2}^{-}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\phantom{-}}(X_{0}^{-})^{T}\\
-(X_{0}^{+})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\right),

where
X_{i}
is the matrix of the linear transformation
X|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}
of the restriction of
X
to
V_{i}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\neq(2m-2)
,
X_{0}^{+}
is the matrix of the restriction of
X_{0}
to
V_{0}^{+}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
,
X_{0}^{-}
is the matrix of the restriction of
X_{0}
to
V_{0}^{-}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{-}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
,
X_{-2}^{+}
is the matrix of the linear transformation obtained from
X_{-2}
followed by the projection of
V_{0}^{+}\oplus V_{0}^{-}
to
V_{0}^{+}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{-2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{+}
and
X_{-2}^{-}
is the matrix of the linear transformation obtained from
X_{-2}
followed by the projection of
V_{0}^{+}\oplus V_{0}^{-}
to
V_{0}^{-}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{-2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{-}
. Above
M^{T}
denotes the transpose of the matrix
M
.

Moreover the subset of elements

(X_{i})_{\begin{subarray}{l}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-2)\end{subarray}}\in\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-2)\end{subarray}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})\right),

such that the above conditions for the
X_{i}
are satisfied, is a subspace of

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\neq(2m-2)\end{subarray}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i+2})

isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
.

(c)

Z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
if and only if all the following conditions are verified

•

Z(V_{i})\subseteq V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

Z_{-i}=-Z_{i}^{T}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq 2
;

•

Z_{0}
is such that
\langle Z_{0}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle v,Z_{0}(v^{\prime})\rangle=0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{0}
; in other words,
Z_{0}
is in the Lie algebra corresponding to
V_{0}
and the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
;

where we denote
Z_{i}
is the matrix of the linear transformation
Z|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
the restriction of
Z
to
V_{i}
relative to the bases
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
.

Moreover the subset of elements

(Z_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in\left(\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i})\right),

such that the above conditions for the
Z_{i}
are satisfied, is a subspace of

\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}Hom(V_{i},V_{i})

isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.

(d)

\{(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}GL(V_{i})\mid g_{-i}=(g_{i}^{*})^{-1}\text{ for all $i>0$ and }g_{0}\in G_{0}\}
is a closed subgroup of
\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}GL(V_{i})
and is isomorphic to
\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}},i>0}GL(V_{i})\times G_{0}
, where
G_{0}
is the orthogonal group
O(V_{0})
(respectively the symplectic group
Sp(V_{0})
).

Moreover the function
