Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v_{0}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime}))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}v_{0}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}),&\text{ if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(b,j)$;}\\
-v_{0}^{j}(\tau(b)),&\text{ if $(b^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=(\tau(b^{\prime}),j^{\prime})$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This means

\xi_{(b_{1},j_{1})}^{(b_{2},j_{2})}=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}1,&\text{ if $(b_{1},j_{1})=(b,j)$ and $(b_{2},j_{2})=(\tau(b^{\prime}),j^{\prime})$;}\\
-1,&\text{ if $(b_{1},j_{1})=(\tau(b^{\prime}),j^{\prime})$ and $(b_{2},j_{2})=(b,j)$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We get easily that

\langle Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v),v^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}+\langle v,Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}(v^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=0

for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{0}
from the equations (
5
). Note that the pair
\tau(b^{\prime}),\tau(b)
falls also in the case (vi) and it is easy to see that
Z_{(\tau(b^{\prime}),j^{\prime})}^{(\tau(b),j)}=-Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}
by evaluating both sides on the basis vectors
v_{0}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
.

By looking on the basis vectors
v_{0}^{j^{\prime\prime}}(b^{\prime\prime})
, we have that

ad(H_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{0}})Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=(h_{0}(b^{\prime})-h_{0}(b))Z_{(b,j)}^{(b^{\prime},j^{\prime})}.

We have used lemma
2.14
(a) in the proof of this formula.

From this, we get in the orthogonal case that

\dim({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime\prime}_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(b,b^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\\
0\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})\\
b\neq\tau(b^{\prime})\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}c(b)c(b^{\prime})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
0\in Supp(b)\\
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(\tau(b))\end{subarray}}c(b)(c(b)-1)\right).

Note that we have also used the fact that
(h_{0}(b^{\prime})-h_{0}(b))\geq 0
is equivalent to
(0-h_{0}(b))\geq(0-h_{0}(b^{\prime}))
; in other words,
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(b^{\prime})
.

From all the formulas above, the proposition follows.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
X
be an element of the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
and let
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
the symmetric function corresponding to
{\mathbf{c}}
. Then the partition corresponding to the Jordan type of
X
is

\prod_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}|Supp(b)|^{c(b)}.

Here we have described the partition in multiplicative form.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the one of proposition
3.17
and is left to the reader.
∎

4.18.

As when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even, we want to give now a compact way to describe the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
corresponding to the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
but this time when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd. In fact, we will give two approachs to describe the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition 4.19
.

A
orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
is the
{\mathcal{I}}
-diagram with its boxes filled with elements of
{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
c_{i,j}
denoted the entry in the row indexed by
i
and in the column indexed by
j
, then we have
c_{-j,-i}=c_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq j
. A
symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
is the
{\mathcal{I}}
-diagram with its boxes filled with elements of
{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
c_{i,j}
denoted the entry in the row indexed by
i
and in the column indexed by
j
, then we have
c_{-j,-i}=c_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq j
with the added condition that
c_{i,-i}
is an even integer for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
(i.e. the entries on the principal diagonal are even).

Note this is different from how we define orthogonal and symplectic symmetric tableau when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even. The two notions are transposed here.

Example 4.20
.

For the
{\mathcal{I}}=\{4,2,0,-2,-4\}
, then

{\mathcal{T}}
=

-4

-2

0

2

4

{\phantom{-}}4

0

{\phantom{-}}2

1

{\phantom{-}}0

2

-2

1

-4

0

is an orthogonal symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau, but not a symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau.

{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
=

-4

-2

0

2

4

{\phantom{-}}4

1

{\phantom{-}}2

1

{\phantom{-}}0

2

-2

1

-4

1

is a symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau. We have kept the indices for the rows and columns inscribed in both cases.

Definition 4.21
.

Given the symplectic (respectively orthogonal) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}=(c_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}
, we define its
dimension vector

\delta({\mathcal{T}})=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, where

\delta_{i}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}\quad\text{for all $i\in{\mathcal{I}}$.}

Example 4.22
.

If
{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
are the symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux of example
4.20
, then their dimension vectors are

\displaystyle\delta({\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{4},\delta_{2},\delta_{0},\delta_{-2},\delta_{-4})=(3,9,15,9,3)\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\delta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{4},\delta_{2},\delta_{0},\delta_{-2},\delta_{-4})=(7,10,16,10,7)

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
: the set of symplectic (respectively orthogonal) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{T}}
of dimension
\delta({\mathcal{T}})=\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
and by
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\delta}
: the set of symplectic (respectively orthogonal) symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{R}}=(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\geq j}
such that

r_{i,i}=\delta_{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad(r_{i,j}-r_{i+2,j}-r_{i,j-2}+r_{i+2,j-2})\geq 0\quad\text{ for all $i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}$ and $i\geq j$}

where we set
r_{i,j}=0
if either
i\not\in{\mathcal{I}}
or
j\not\in{\mathcal{I}}
or both. Then the map
\varTheta:{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{R}}_{\delta}

\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})=\varTheta((c_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j})=(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\geq j}\quad\text{where $r_{i,j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq j\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}$}

for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
, is a well-defined bijection.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of lemma
3.23
and is left to the reader.
∎

.

-tableau of example

. Then

Definition 4.25
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and the coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. To
{\mathbf{c}}
, denote the corresponding symmetric function by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. We define the symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
as follows: if
c_{i,j}
is the entry at the row
i
and column
j
with
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\geq j
, then

c_{i,j}=\begin{cases}c(b(i,j,0)),&\text{ if $i+j\neq 0$;}\\
c(b(i,j,0)),&\text{ if $i+j=0$ in the orthogonal case;}\\
c(b(i,j,0))+c(b(i,j,1)),&\text{ if $i+j=0$ in the symplectic case.}\end{cases}

Because
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is symmetric,
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is clearly a symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau. Moreover in the symplectic case, because we have that
c(b(i,j,0))=c(b(i,j,1))
when
i+j=0
, we get that
c_{i,-i}
is even. Thus
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is an symplectic symmetric
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\end{subarray}}c_{u,v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq v\\
u+v\neq 0\end{subarray}}c(b(u,v,0))

\displaystyle+\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-u\end{subarray}}(c(b(u,-u,0))+c(b(u,-u,1)))\text{ in the symplectic case;}\\
\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
u\geq i\geq-u\end{subarray}}c(b(u,-u,0))\text{ in the orthogonal case;}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
i\in Supp(b)\end{subarray}}c(b)=\delta_{i}.

Consequently
\delta({\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}}))=\delta
and
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
\delta_{-i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}
denotes the set of
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. Then

(a)

the map
{\mathcal{T}}:{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
, where
{\mathbf{c}}\mapsto{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}})
is a bijection;

(b)

the map
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/G^{\iota}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}\quad\text{ defined by }\quad G^{\iota}\cdot X\mapsto{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbf{c}}_{X})
is a well-defined bijection.

Proof.

(a) is obvious from the definition of
{\mathcal{T}}
above and (b) is a corollary of proposition
4.12

∎

Notation 4.27
.

We will denote the
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
corresponding to the
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableau
{\mathcal{T}}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
as defined in the bijection of lemma
4.26
(b) by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}

In
[
2
]
, M. Boos and G. Cerulli Irelli, were able to describe the partial order on the
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
for symmetric quivers in the orthogonal and symplectic cases for symmetric quivers of type
A_{m}^{even}
and
A_{m}^{odd}
. We will now describe their result in our case (i.e.
|{\mathcal{I}}|
odd).

Proposition 4.28

(Boos and Cerulli Irelli)
.

(In the situation of
4.2
) Let
{\mathcal{I}}
-tableaux
{\mathcal{T}}
,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\delta}
and the corresponding
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
. Then the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is contained in the Zariski closure of the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}
if and only if

(r_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}={\mathcal{R}}=\varTheta({\mathcal{T}})\leq\varTheta({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}=(r^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i,j\in{\mathcal{I}},i\geq j}

where the inequality here between the tableaux
{\mathcal{R}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
means
r_{i,j}\leq r^{\prime}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of the proposition
3.28
and is left to the reader.
∎

Example 4.30
.

4.2
, let

,
G

V
over
{\mathbf{k}}

,
{\mathcal{I}}=\{4,2,0,-2,-4\}
,
\dim(V_{0})=3=\delta_{0}
,
\dim(V_{2})=2=\delta_{2}=\delta_{-2}
,
\dim(V_{4})=1=\delta_{4}=\delta_{-4}
. In other words,

and
\iota(t)
on

.

5.
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
in the special orthogonal case when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd.

5.1.

In this section, we will restrict ourself to the special orthogonal case when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd. The special orthogonal case is trickier. The reason being that special orthogonal isomorphism is more restricted that orthogonal isomorphism as we saw in
2.22
.

5.2.

In this section,

•

m
is an integer
>0
;

•

\tilde{G}
is the group of automorphisms of a finite dimensional vector space
V
over
k
preserving a fixed non-degenerate symmetric form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:V\times V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
; in other words,
\tilde{G}=O(V)
;

•

G
is the connected component of the identity element of the group
\tilde{G}
, in other words,
G=SO(V)
;

•

\epsilon=1
;

•

\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
, more precisely
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if
X:V\rightarrow V
is an endomorphism of
V
such that
\langle X(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle+\langle v_{1},X(v_{2})\rangle=0
for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
;

•

V
is identified to its dual
V^{*}
by the isomorphism
\phi:V\rightarrow V^{*}
defined by
v\mapsto\phi_{v}
, where
\phi_{v}:V\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
is
\phi_{v}(x)=\langle v,x\rangle
for all
x\in V
;

•

{\mathcal{I}}=\{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mid n\equiv 0\pmod{2},-2m<n<2m\}
;

•

\oplus_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}V_{i}
is a direct sum decomposition of
V
by vector subspaces
V_{i}\neq 0
such that

–

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0
whenever
v\in V_{i}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{j}
and
i+j\neq 0
;

–

V_{-i}=V_{i}^{*}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
under the identification given by
\phi
above.

Note that the restriction of the non-degenerate symmetric form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
to
V_{0}\times V_{0}
is a non-degenerate symmetric form.

•

{\mathcal{B}}=\coprod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
is a basis of
V
such that each
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\{u_{i,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
is a basis of
V_{i}
for each
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
i\neq 0
, where
\delta_{i}
is the dimension of
V_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=\{u_{0,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{0}\}
is a basis of
V_{0}
, where
\delta_{0}
is the dimension of
V_{0}
and these bases are such that, for
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,

\displaystyle\langle u_{i,r},u_{j,s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $i,j\neq 0$, $i+j=0$ and $r=s$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\langle u_{0,r},u_{0,s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $r+s=\delta_{0}+1$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle\langle u_{0,r},u_{i,s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u_{i,s},u_{0,r}\rangle=0,\text{ if $i\neq 0$, $1\leq s\leq\delta_{i}$, $1\leq r\leq\delta_{0}$.}

•

\iota:{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}\rightarrow{\tilde{G}}
is the homomorphism defined by
\iota(t)v=t^{i}v
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
v\in V_{i}
and
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
and clearly
\iota(t)\in G
for all
t\in{\mathbf{k}}^{\times}
;

•

{\mathbf{B}}
is the set of
{\mathcal{I}}
-boxes;

•

{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
denote the set of
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
in
{\mathbf{B}}
;

•

\nu=|{\mathbf{B}}/\tau|=m^{2}
.

5.3.

In proposition
4.12
, we gave a parametrization of the set
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
of
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
by giving a bijection
\Upsilon:{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}/{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. To
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
, we have denoted the corresponding
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit under
\Upsilon
by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. From our observation in
4.15
and lemma
4.3
(a), we have that the indice
[{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}:G^{\iota}]=[O(V_{0}):SO(V_{0})]=2
. Note here we used the fact that
\dim(V_{0})\neq 0
. Consequently the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is either a
G^{\iota}
-orbit or splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits. We need to give a criteria on
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
for when the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
splits or not.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
and the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
associated to
{\mathbf{c}}
.

(a)

There exists
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0\geq j
such that
c(b(i,j,0))>0
.

(b)

If there is an element
i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
c(b(i^{\prime},-i^{\prime},0))>0
, then the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
does not split and it is a unique
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
.

(c)

If
c(b(i,-i,0))=0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
, then the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits:
{\ }{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\ }{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Moreover we have that
\dim({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})=\dim({\ }^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})=\dim({\ }^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})

Proof.

(a) We have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
0\in Supp(b)\end{subarray}}c(b)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\geq 0\geq j\end{subarray}}c(b(i,j,0))=\delta_{0}>0

and the result follows easily, because
c(b)\geq 0
for all
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
.

(b) Let
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
be the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism such that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
constructed in proposition
4.12
. Using this isomorphism we saw that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit of
T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T_{\mathbf{c}}^{-1}
. We will write this element
T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T_{\mathbf{c}}^{-1}
as
X_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
. Thus
X_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation of degree 2 such that
\langle X_{\mathbf{c}}(u),v\rangle+\langle u,X_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=0
for all
u,v\in V
. Let
X:V\rightarrow V
be an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation of degree 2 such that
\langle X(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle+\langle v_{1},X(v_{2}) \rangle=0
for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
and
X\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Thus there exists
{\tilde{g}}\in{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
such that
Ad({\tilde{g}})(X_{\mathbf{c}})=X
.

If we consider
X_{\mathbf{c}}
,
X
and
{\tilde{g}}
as in lemma
4.3
(a) and (b); in other words, if we write
X_{{\mathbf{c}},i}:X_{\mathbf{c}}|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}
and
X_{i}:X|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i+2}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq 2(m-1)
and
{\tilde{g}}_{i}={\tilde{g}}|_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have
X_{i}\circ{\tilde{g}}_{i}={\tilde{g}}_{i+2}\circ X_{{\mathbf{c}},i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq 2(m-1)
. We have
{\tilde{g}}_{i}\in GL(V_{i})
,
\det({\tilde{g}}_{-i})=(\det({\tilde{g}}_{i}))^{-1}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\neq 0
,
{\tilde{g}}_{0}\in O(V_{0})
and
\det({\tilde{g}})=\det({\tilde{g}}_{0})
.

By hypothesis, there exists an
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
such that
b^{\prime}=b(i^{\prime},-i^{\prime},0)
and
c(b^{\prime})\neq 0
, where
i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i^{\prime}\geq 0
. The
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b^{\prime}
is
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=\{b^{\prime}\}
. Consider the symmetric function
c^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
defined by

c^{\prime}(b)=\begin{cases}c(b),&\text{if $b\neq b^{\prime}$;}\\
(c(b^{\prime})-1),&\text{if $b=b^{\prime}$.}\end{cases}

and the associated coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. From its definition in
4.7
, we have that
V({\mathbf{c}})
is the direct sum of
V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}})
and one copy of
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
. Moreover the orthogonal complement
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\perp}
of
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
is
V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}})
. Because
b^{\prime}
is on the principal diagonal and from our construction for the orthogonal representation of
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})=V(b^{\prime})
, then we know that the
{\mathcal{I}}
-component
V_{i}(b^{\prime})
of
V(b^{\prime})
for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
are 1-dimensional for
i\in Supp(b^{\prime})
and 0-dimensional otherwise; in particular
\dim(V_{0}(b^{\prime}))=1
.

Using the isomorphism
T_{\mathbf{c}}
, we get that
V
is the direct sum of the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded indecomposable orthogonal representation
U=T_{\mathbf{c}}(V(b^{\prime}))
and its orthogonal complement
U^{\perp}=T_{\mathbf{c}}(V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}))
such that
U_{i}
is 1-dimensional exactly when
i\in Supp(b^{\prime})
and 0-dimensional otherwise. We want to show that there exists an element
g
of
G^{\iota}
such that
Ad(g)(X_{\mathbf{c}})=X
. If the element
{\tilde{g}}
above is such that
{\tilde{g}}
of
G^{\iota}
, then we are done by taking
{\tilde{g}}
for
g
.

Assume now that
{\tilde{g}}\not\in G^{\iota}
, in other words,
\det({\tilde{g}}_{0})=-1
. Consider the linear transformation
h:V\rightarrow V
such that
h(v)=h(u+u^{\perp})=-u+u^{\perp}
, where
v=u+u^{\perp}
, with
u\in U
and
u^{\perp}\in U^{\perp}
. It is easy to see that
h
preserve the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
and that
h\in{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
. Also we have that
Ad(h)(X_{\mathbf{c}})=X_{\mathbf{c}}
, because

\displaystyle Ad(h)X_{\mathbf{c}}(v)

\displaystyle=hX_{\mathbf{c}}h^{-1}(u+u^{\perp})=hX_{\mathbf{c}}(-u+u^{\perp})=h(-X_{\mathbf{c}}(u)+X_{\mathbf{c}}(u^{\perp}))

\displaystyle=X_{\mathbf{c}}(u)+X_{\mathbf{c}}(u^{\perp})=X_{\mathbf{c}}(v),

where
v=u+u^{\perp}
, with
u\in U
and
u^{\perp}\in U^{\perp}
.

Consider now
g={\tilde{g}}h\in{\tilde{G}}
. We have
Ad(g)(X_{\mathbf{c}})=Ad({\tilde{g}}h)(X_{\mathbf{c}})=Ad({\tilde{g}})(X_{\mathbf{c}})=X
and
\det(g)=\det({\tilde{g}}_{0})\det(h_{0})=1
, because
\det({\tilde{g}}_{0})=-1
and
\det(h_{0})=-1
from the fact that
\dim(U_{0})=1
and the definition of
h
. So
g\in G^{\iota}
and the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is a unique
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
.

(c) We will prove this by contradiction. Assume there exist a dimension vector
\delta=(\delta_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
, a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
and the associated symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
such that

•

\delta_{i}>0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

c(b(i,-i,0))=0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
;

•

the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is a
G^{\iota}
-orbit, in other words the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
does not split;

•

the sum

\sum_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})

is minimal among all of the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
as above.

Let
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}=\{b\in{\mathbf{B}}\mid b=b(i,j,0)\text{ for some $i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}$, $i\geq 0\geq j$ and $i+j\neq 0$}\}
. By (a) and, because of our hypothesis,
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and there exist
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
with
i^{\prime}\geq 0\geq j^{\prime}
,
i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}>0
such that
b^{\prime}=b(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0)\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
c(b^{\prime})>0
. Note that
0\in Supp(b^{\prime})
and
0\in Supp(\tau(b^{\prime}))
. Denote by
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b^{\prime}
. So
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=\{b^{\prime},\tau(b^{\prime})\}
.

Consider the symmetric function
c^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
defined by

c^{\prime}(b)=\begin{cases}c(b),&\text{if $b\not\in\{b^{\prime},\tau(b^{\prime})\}$;}\\
(c(b^{\prime})-1),&\text{if $b\in\{b^{\prime},\tau(b^{\prime})\}$.}\end{cases}

and the associated coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. From its definition in
4.7
, we have that
V({\mathbf{c}})
is the direct sum of
V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}})
and one copy of
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
. More precise,

V({\mathbf{c}})
: the direct sum as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded vector space and as
\epsilon
-representations of
{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of
V({\mathcal{O}})
with a
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}=\coprod_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\coprod_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})\}.

and the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
is described in
4.7
with
\epsilon=1
. But
V({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})
: the direct sum as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded vector space and as orthogonal representations of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})
copies of
V({\mathcal{O}})
with a
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}=\coprod_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\coprod_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})\}.

and the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
is obtained by restriction of
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
to
V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}})
and
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
as
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded vector space and as orthogonal representation with a
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis
\{v_{i}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime}),\ v_{i}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))\mid i\in Supp(b^{\prime})\}
and the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is obtained by restriction of
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
to
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
. Note that the orthogonal complement
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\perp}
of
V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
is
V({{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}})
.

Let
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
be the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformation of degree
0
defined by

S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(u)=\begin{cases}u,&\text{if $u\in V({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})$;}\\
u,&\text{if $u\in V_{i}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})$ and $i\neq 0$;}\\
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime})),&\text{if $u=v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime})$;}\\
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime}),&\text{if $u=v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))$.}\end{cases}

Let
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
be the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism such that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
constructed in proposition
4.12
.
We can now consider the two
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded linear transformations
E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
and
E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}^{-1}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
and their corresponding elements
X_{\mathbf{c}}=T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T^{-1}_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
and
X^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}=T_{\mathbf{c}}E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}T^{-1}_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
. Using this isomorphism, we saw that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit of
X_{\mathbf{c}}
, but also the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit of
X^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}
. From our construction, both of these are in the same
G^{\iota}
-orbit. Thus by our assumption, there exists a special orthogonal element
g\in G^{\iota}
such that
Ad(g)X_{\mathbf{c}}=X^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}
, i.e.
det(g)=1
.

By considering the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded vector subspace
U=T_{\mathbf{c}}(V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}))
and its orthogonal complement
U^{\perp}=T_{\mathbf{c}}(V({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}))
, we get that both are
Ad(g)
-invariant subspaces. In this case,
\det(g)=\det(g|_{U})\det(g|_{U^{\perp}})=1
and by the minimality of
{\mathbf{c}}
, we have that
\det(g|_{U^{\perp}})=1
. Thus
\det(g|_{U})=1
, but this contradicts the fact we saw in
2.20
and
2.22
that
E_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}|_{V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})}E_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}S^{-1}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}|_{V({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})}
are not isomorphic relative to the special orthogonal group. This completes the proof that the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. With the above notation,
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
G^{\iota}
-orbit of
X_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
G^{\iota}
-orbit of
X^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

If there is at least one
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
that splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits, then the dimension
\dim(V)
of
V
(equivalently
\dim(V_{0})
of
V_{0}
) is even. This follows easily from the fact that, if
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits, then
c(b(i,-i,0))=0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
for the symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
corresponding to
{\mathbf{c}}
by proposition
5.4
(c). Then

\displaystyle\delta_{0}

\displaystyle=\dim(V_{0})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\geq 0\geq j\end{subarray}}c(b(i,j,0))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\geq 0\geq j\\
i+j\neq 0\end{subarray}}c(b(i,j,0))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\geq 0\end{subarray}}c(b(i,-i,0))

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
i\geq 0\geq j\\
i+j>0\end{subarray}}c(b(i,j,0))

because, if
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0\geq j
and
i+j>0
, then
c(b(i,j,0))=c(b(-j,-i,0))
.

Notation 5.6
.

Using the same notation as in proposition
5.4
, we can associated to each
G^{\iota}
-orbit: a Jacobson-Morozov triple.

•

If the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
does not split (case (b) in the proposition
5.4
), then the Jacobson-Morozov triple associated to the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is
(E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}})
as in
4.7
.

•

If the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
does split (case (c) in proposition
5.4
) and the
G^{\iota}
-orbit is
{\ }{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
, then the Jacobson-Morozov triple associated to the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\ }{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is
(E_{\mathbf{c}},H_{\mathbf{c}},F_{\mathbf{c}})
as in
4.7
. We will denote this triple as
({\ }^{\prime}E_{\mathbf{c}},{\ }^{\prime}H_{\mathbf{c}},{\ }^{\prime}F_{\mathbf{c}})
.

•

If the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
does split (case (c) in proposition
5.4
) and the
G^{\iota}
-orbit is
{\ }{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
, then the Jacobson-Morozov triple associated to the
G^{\iota}
-orbit

{\ }{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
will be denoted as
({\ }^{\prime\prime}E_{\mathbf{c}},{\ }^{\prime\prime}H_{\mathbf{c}},{\ }^{\prime\prime}F_{\mathbf{c}})
. If
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}/\tau
is as in the proof of proposition
5.4
(c), where
{}^{\prime\prime}E_{\mathbf{c}}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}S^{-1}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
,
{}^{\prime\prime}H_{\mathbf{c}}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}H_{\mathbf{c}}S^{-1}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}F_{\mathbf{c}}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}F_{\mathbf{c}}S^{-1}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
. Note that if we had chosen another
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}/\tau
is as in the proof of proposition
5.4
(c), then we get another Jacobson-Morozov triple, but it is in the same
G(V_{\mathbf{c}})^{\iota}
-orbit.

Using the isomorphism
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V_{\mathbf{c}}\rightarrow V
, we can transport an associated Jacobson-Morozov triple to a
G^{\iota}
-orbit to the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Example 5.7
.

If we reconsider the example
4.30
, then none of the fourteen
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbits splits because the dimension
\dim(V_{0})=3
of
V_{0}
is odd. Consequently all fourteen
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbits are
G^{\iota}
-orbits.

Figure 5
.
Poset obtained by the rank tableaux comparaison

We have illustrated the Hasse diagram of the poset obtained when we compare the rank tableaux
{\mathcal{R}}
for the set of orbits in figure
5
. We could described each of these
G^{\iota}
-orbits as follows: an element of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
is given by the quiver representation

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{k}}^{2}@<{X_{0}}<{}<{\mathbf{k}}^{2}@<{X_{-2}}<{}<{\mathbf{k}}^{2}\end{CD}

where
X_{0}
and
X_{-2}
are the matrices

X_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad X_{-2}=-\begin{bmatrix}d&b\\
c&a\end{bmatrix}.

Then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{4}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid(ad-bc)\neq 0\};

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid(ad-bc)=0\text{ and either $ad\neq 0$ or $bc\neq 0$ or $ab\neq 0$ or $cd\neq 0$}\};

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid\text{$ad=bc=ab=cd=0$ and ($a,b,c,d)\neq(0,0,0,0)$}\}

\displaystyle{\ }{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid\text{$a=c=0$ and $(b,d)\neq(0,0)$}\}

\displaystyle{\ }{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid\text{$b=d=0$ and $(a,c)\neq(0,0)$}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(a,b,c,d)\in{\mathbf{k}}^{4}\mid a=b=c=d=0\}

We have included in this list the
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
and how it splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits:
{\ }{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
and
{\ }{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
.

Remark 5.9
.

It would be interesting to know what is the Zariski closure of the
G^{\iota}
-orbits in this case. The article of Boos and Cerulli Irelli
[
2
]
does not address this question.

6.
Conditions for two
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
to be in the same
G
-orbit.

6.1.

In this section, we want to answer the question of when two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
are in the same
G
-orbit where
G
is either the reductive group
Sp(V)
(respectively
SO(V)
) as in
3.1
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even or the reductive group
Sp(V)
(respectively
O(V)
) as in
4.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd or finally the reductive group
SO(V)
as in
5.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd.

Let two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
whose partitions corresponding to their Jordan type are respectively
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
and
1^{a^{\prime}(1)}2^{a^{\prime}(2)}3^{a^{\prime}(3)}\dots
. If these two orbits are in the same
G
-orbit, then these two partitions will be equal (i.e.
a(k)=a^{\prime}(k)
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
). This follows easily from the fact that they are
GL(V)
-conjugate.

Thus in this section, we will consider two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
that have the same partition corresponding to their Jordan type
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
and we will answer the question of when they are
G
-conjugate.

6.2.

By propositions
3.17
and
4.17
, we know the partition corresponding to the Jordan type for the elements in a
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
and consequently in their
G
-orbit. It is known when the characteristic
p
is 0 or is sufficiently large that the nilpotent class in the Lie algebra of
Sp(V)
(respectively
O(V)
) is the intersection of the nilpotent class of the Lie algebra of
GL(V)
with the Lie algebra of
Sp(V)
(respectively
O(V)
). Moreover a nilpotent class of
O(V)
in its Lie algebra is also a nilpotent class of
SO(V)
in its Lie algebra except when the partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
(in multiplicative form) corresponding to its Jordan type is such that
a(k)=0
when
k\equiv 1\pmod{2}
and
a(k)\equiv 0\pmod{2}
when
k\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. We will say that these latter partitions are
totally even
. See for example section 2.5 in
[
12
]
. In this reference, it is quoted for unipotent classes, but it is also valid for the nilpotent classes when
p
is
0
or sufficiently large.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
Sp(V)
(respectively
O(V)
) in the situation either as in
3.1
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even or as in
4.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd and let
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
be two
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
who both have the same partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
corresponding to their Jordan type. Then these two
G^{\iota}
-orbits are in the same
G
-orbit.

Proof.

If their corresponding partitions are equal, then they are
GL(V)
-conjugate and by our observation in
6.2
they are
Sp(V)
-conjugate (resp.
O(V)
-conjugate).
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
SO(V)
in the situation either as in
3.1
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even or as in
5.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd and let
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
be two
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
who both have the same partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
corresponding to their Jordan type and this partition is not totally even. Then these two
G^{\iota}
-orbits are in the same
G
-orbit.

Proof.

If their corresponding partitions are equal, then they are
GL(V)
-conjugate and by our observation in
6.2
and because the partition is not totally even, then they are
SO(V)
-conjugate.
∎

6.5.

So we are left to study the case of the reductive group
G=SO(V)
and two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, that have the same partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
corresponding to their Jordan type and this partition is totally even, i.e. the partition is of the form
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
with
a(k)\equiv 0\pmod{2}
when
k\equiv 0\pmod{2}
.

In the case where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd and
{\tilde{G}}
denotes the group
O(V)
, if
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
denotes a
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
is a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is the associated symmetric function to
{\mathbf{c}}
, such that
c(b(i,-i,0))>0
for at least one element
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
, then, by proposition
5.4
(b), we know that this
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is a
G^{\iota}
-orbit, i.e. does not splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits:
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Moreover the partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
corresponding to the Jordan type of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is not totally even. This last fact follows because
|Supp(b(i,-i,0))|
is odd for the
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b(i,-i,0)
on the principal diagonal such that
c(b(i,-i,0))>0
, where
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
.

In the case where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd, if
{\tilde{G}}
denotes the group
O(V)
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
denotes a
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
is a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is the associated symmetric function to
{\mathbf{c}}
, such that
c(b(i,-i,0))=0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
, then, by proposition
5.4
(c), we know that this
{\tilde{G}}^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
splits into two
G^{\iota}
-orbits:
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. By the preceding paragraph, it is among these
G^{\iota}
-orbits that we must look for
G^{\iota}
-orbits whose partition
1^{a(1)}2^{a(2)}3^{a(3)}\dots
corresponding to the Jordan type could be totally even.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
SO(V)
in the situation as in
3.1
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even (respectively as in
5.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd) and let
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
(respectively
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
or
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
) be a
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
whose partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
corresponding to its Jordan type is totally even, where
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Denote by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
: the symmetric function associated to
{\mathbf{c}}
. If
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
is such that
\lambda(b)\not\equiv|{\mathcal{I}}|\pmod{2}
, then
c(b)=0
. Recall that
\lambda(b)=(i+j)/2
for
b=b(i,j,k)
, with
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
and
0\leq k\leq\mu(i,j)
as defined in
2.18
.

Proof.

In both cases:
|{\mathcal{I}}|
even or odd, the result follows easily from the fact that if
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
, then
|Supp(b)|\equiv(\lambda(b)+|{\mathcal{I}}|)\pmod{2}
and the partition corresponding to the Jordan type is totally even.
∎

Notation 6.7
.

With the same notation and hypothesis as the above lemma, we get that if
c(b)\neq 0
for a symmetric function
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, then
|Supp(b)|
is even. For
b=b(i,j,k)
with
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq j
and
0\leq k\leq\mu(i,j)
, we denote by

Supp^{top}(b)=\left\{i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\ \left|\ i\geq i^{\prime}\geq\left(\frac{i+j+2}{2}\right)\right.\right\}

This is the top-half of the elements in the support
Supp(b)
of the
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
.

We will consider separately the situation in
3.1
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even and the one in
5.2
when
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
SO(V)
as in
3.1
where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even and let
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
be a
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, whose partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
corresponding to its Jordan type is totally even, where
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Denote by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
: the symmetric function associated to
{\mathbf{c}}
. We will now recall the notation of
3.6
.
V({\mathbf{c}})
is a vector space with its
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}=\coprod_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\coprod_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}(b)\}

and the non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\times V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
,
E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
is also defined in
3.6
. It is a nilpotent element whose partition corresponding to the Jordan type is the even partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
. Then the vector subspace

L({\mathbf{c}})=E_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{2}_{\mathbf{c}})]+E^{2}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{4}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots+E^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots

is the vector subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
spanned by the basis

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}

and this is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
.

Proof.

Recall that the linear transformation
E_{\mathbf{c}}
on the elements of the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
was described in notation
3.6
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b
, then, if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has no overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{ if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i>0$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i<0$;}\end{cases}

while if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
b=b(i_{1},j_{1},k_{1})\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, then we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{ if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i\geq 1$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i<-1$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is below the principal diagonal;}\\
-v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is above the principal diagonal;}\\
(-1)^{k_{1}}v_{1}^{j}(b),&\text{ if $i=-1$ and $b$ is on the principal diagonal.}\end{cases}

Let
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
be an
{\mathcal{I}}
-box such that
c(b)\neq 0
. As noted in
6.7
, then
|Supp(b)|
is even. Write this cardinality as
2s
.

From our definition of
E_{\mathbf{c}}
, we get easily that

(i)

for
1\leq k\leq s
and
1\leq j\leq c(b)
, then

E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=0\iff i>(\max(Supp(b))-4k);

(ii)

for
k>s
and
1\leq j\leq c(b)
, then
E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=0
for all
i\in Supp(b)
.

If
1\leq k\leq s
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
i>(\max(Supp(b))-4k)
, then
v_{i}^{j}(b)\in\ker(E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})
and

E^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))\text{ is }\begin{cases}0,&\text{if $(i+2k)>\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
\gamma v_{i+2k}^{j}(b),&\text{if $(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))$;}\end{cases}

where
\gamma\in\{1,-1\}
.

In this second case, i.e.
(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))
, then
(i+2k)\in Supp^{top}(b)
, because
Supp(b)=\{i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid\max(Supp(b))\geq i^{\prime}\geq\min(Supp(b))\}
, with
\min(Supp(b))=(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2)
and

Supp^{top}(b)=\{i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid\max(Supp(b))\geq i^{\prime}\geq(\max(Supp(b))-2s+2)\}.

So in this second case, we have
(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))
and also the lower inequality
(i+2k)>(\max(Supp(b))-2k)\geq(\max(Supp(b))-2s)
, because
1\leq k\leq s
. This show that
(i+2k)\in Supp^{top}(b)
.

If
k>s
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
i\in Supp(b)
, then
v_{i}^{j}(b)\in\ker(E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})
and

E^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))\text{ is }\begin{cases}0,&\text{if $(i+2k)>\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
\gamma v_{i+2k}^{j}(b),&\text{if $(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))$;}\end{cases}

where
\gamma\in\{1,-1\}
.

In this second case, i.e.
(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))
, then
(i+2k)\in Supp^{top}(b)
. because we have
(i+2k)\leq\max(Supp(b))
and also the lower inequality
(i+2k)\geq(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2+2k)>(\max(Supp(b))-2s+2)
, because
k>s
. This show that
(i+2k)\in Supp^{top}(b)
.

Thus
L({\mathbf{c}})
is included in the subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
spanned by

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}.

Reciprocally if
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
c(b)>0
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
and
i\in Supp^{top}(b)
, then we want to prove that
v_{i}^{j}(b)\in L({\mathbf{c}})
. Write again
|Supp(b)|=2s
and we have that
Supp(b)=\{i^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid\max(Supp(b))\geq i^{\prime}\geq\min(Supp(b))\}
, with
\min(Supp(b))=(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2)
. Because
i\in Supp^{top}(b)
, this means that
\max(Supp(b))\geq i\geq(\max(Supp(b))-2s+2)
. Write
i=\max(Supp(b))-2r
with
0\leq r\leq(s-1)
. We have that

\displaystyle\max(Supp(b))\geq(i-2r-2)

\displaystyle\geq(\max(Supp(b))-4r-2)

\displaystyle\geq(\max(Supp(b))-4(s-1)-2=\min(Supp(b)).

Thus
(i-2r-2)\in Supp(b)
and we have
E^{(r+1)}_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{(i-2r-2)}^{j}(b))=\gamma v_{i}^{j}(b)
, where
\gamma\in\{1,-1\}
.
Note that
v_{(i-2r-2)}^{j}(b)\in\ker E_{\mathbf{c}}^{2(r+1)}
, because
(i-2r-2)+4(r+1)=(\max(Supp(b))-4r-2)+4(r+1)> \max(Supp(b))
.
From all of the above,
L({\mathbf{c}})
is the vector subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
spanned by the basis

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}.

The dimension
\dim(L({\mathbf{c}}))
of
L({\mathbf{c}})
is

\dim(L({\mathbf{c}}))=\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|Supp^{top}(b)|=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|Supp(b)|=\frac{1}{2}\dim(V({\mathbf{c}})).

We will now show that
L({\mathbf{c}})
is an isotropic subspace. From our construction of the bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
in notation
3.6
, we have
\langle v_{i}^{j}(b),v_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})\rangle\neq 0
only if
j^{\prime}=j
,
b^{\prime}=\tau(b)
and
i+i^{\prime}=0
. Let
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
with
c(b)>0
,
1\leq j\leq c(b)
,
i\in Supp^{top}(b)
,
b^{\prime}=\tau(b)
,
j^{\prime}=j
and
i^{\prime}\in Supp^{top}(b^{\prime})
. Write
|Supp(b)|=2s
. As above

Supp(b)=\{i^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2)\leq i^{\prime\prime}\leq\max(Supp(b))\}.

Thus
|Supp(\tau(b))|=2s
and

Supp(\tau(b))=\{i^{\prime\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid-\max(Supp(b))\leq i^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq-(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2)\}.

From this we get that

Supp^{top}(b)=\{i^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid\max(Supp(b))\geq i^{\prime\prime}\geq(\max(Supp(b))-2s+2)\}

and

Supp^{top}(\tau(b))=\{i^{\prime\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}\mid-(\max(Supp(b))-4s+2)\geq i^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq-(\max(Supp(b))-2s)\}

Now if
i\in Supp^{top}(b)
and
i^{\prime}\in Supp^{top}(\tau(b))
, then

(\max(Supp(b))-2s+2)\leq i\leq\max(Supp(b))

and

(-\max(Supp(b))+2s)\leq i^{\prime}\leq(-\max(Supp(b))+4s-2).

From this, we get that
(i+i^{\prime})\geq 2
and consequently
(i+i^{\prime})\neq 0
and
L({\mathbf{c}})
is an isotropic subspace. It is a maximal isotropic subspace, because
2\dim(L({\mathbf{c}}))=\dim(V({\mathbf{c}}))
.
∎

The following lemma is probably well-known. Its proof is included for completeness.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be even and using the notation of
3.1
, consider the set
{\mathcal{J}}
of subsets
J
of
\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
such that if
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j)\in J
, then
i+i^{\prime}\neq 0
and
J
is maximal among such subsets.

(a)

If
J\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then
2|J|=\dim(V)
.

(b)

If
J\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then the subspace
L_{J}
of
V
spanned by
\{u_{i,j}\mid(i,j)\in J\}
is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V
.

(c)

Let
J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{J}}
. Then the two maximal isotropic subspaces
L_{J}
and
L_{J^{\prime}}
of
V
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit if and only if

|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|\equiv|\{(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid i<0\}|\pmod{2}.

Proof.

(a) From our notation in
3.1
, we have that

\dim(V)=\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\delta_{i}=\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}|\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}|

If
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
is such that
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
, then, by the maximality of
J
, exactly one and only one of
(i,j)
,
(-i,j)
belongs to
J
. Consequently
2|J |=\dim(V)
.

(b) First we will show that
L_{J}
is isotropic. From our hypothesis on the basis as defined in
3.1
, we have that
\langle u_{i,j},u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\rangle\neq 0
if and only if
j=j^{\prime}
and
i+i^{\prime}=0
when
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
are such that
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\delta_{i^{\prime}}
. But by our hypothesis on
J
, when
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in J
, then
\langle u_{i,j},u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\rangle=0
. Otherwise we would have
j=j^{\prime}
and
i+i^{\prime}=0
. But this is impossible for two elements of
J
. So
L_{J}
is isotropic. Because
2\dim(L_{J})=\dim(V)
by (a), we get that
L_{J}
is maximal.

(c) We first need to remind some facts. Let
J_{+}=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid i>0,1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
. Clearly
J_{+}\in{\mathcal{J}}
. Note that if
g\in O(V)
is such that
g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J_{+}}
, then
g\in SO(V)
. In fact by considering the matrix of
g
in the basis
\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in{\mathcal{I}},1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
ordered properly, we get that this matrix is of the form

\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ such that }\quad\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}^{T}\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}.

Consequently
A^{T}D=I
,
\det(D)=(\det(A))^{-1}
and
g\in SO(V)
.

Let
g_{J}:V\rightarrow V
denote the unique linear transformation such that

g_{J}(u_{i,j})=\begin{cases}u_{i,j},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\in J$:}\\
u_{-i,j},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$;}\\
u_{-i,j},&\text{if $i<0$ and $(i,j)\in J$;}\\
u_{i,j},&\text{if $i<0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

We can now show that
g_{J}\in O(V)
. For
i_{1},i_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{i_{1}}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq\delta_{i_{2}}
, we must consider
\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2})}\rangle
. We have

\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $j_{1}=j_{2}$ and $i_{1}+i_{2}=0$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=0,

where
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
and
i^{\prime}_{2}\in\{i_{2},-i_{2}\}
, because
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.

If
j_{1}=j_{2}
and
i_{1}+i_{2}=0
, then we can assume by symmetry that
i_{1}>0
and
i_{2}=-i_{1}<0
. Moreover
(i_{1},j_{1})\in J
if and only if
(i_{2},j_{2})\not\in J
. Thus

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\begin{cases}\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle,&\text{if $(i_{1},j_{1})\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{-i_{1},j_{1}},u_{-i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle,&\text{if $(i_{1},j_{1})\not\in J$;}\end{cases}\quad=1.

If
j_{1}=j_{2}
,
i_{1}+i_{2}\neq 0
and
i_{1}-i_{2}\neq 0
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=0

because
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
,
i^{\prime}_{2}\in\{i_{2},-i_{2}\}
and
i^{\prime}_{1}+i^{\prime}_{2}\neq 0
from our hypothesis.

Finally if
j_{1}=j_{2}
,
i_{1}+i_{2}\neq 0
and
i_{1}=i_{2}
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}}\rangle=0

where
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
.

This completes the proof that
g_{J}\in O(V)
. Note also that we have
g_{J}(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}
, because we only have to consider the basis element
u_{i,j}
for
i>0
and in that case we get if
(i,j)\not\in J
, that
(-i,j)\in J
and

g_{J}(u_{i,j})=\begin{cases}u_{i,j}\in L_{J},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\in J$:}\\
u_{-i,j}\in L_{J},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

We can now compute the determinant of the matrix of
g_{J}
relative to the basis
\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in{\mathcal{I}},1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
where we consider first the basis elements
u_{i,j}
for
(i,j)\in J_{+}
and then the basis element
u_{-i,j}
for
(i,j)\in J_{+}
using the same total order for
J_{+}
. Using the Laplace expansion of
\det(g_{J})
and using recursion, we get easily that

\det(g_{J})=(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|}

If
g\in O(V)
and
g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}
, then
\det(g)=(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|}
, because

g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}=g_{J}(L_{J_{+}})\quad\Rightarrow\quad g^{-1}_{J}g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J_{+}}\quad\text{ and }\quad g^{-1}_{J}g\in SO(V)

and
\det(g)=\det(g_{J})
.

We complete the proof of (c). If
L_{J}
and
L_{J^{\prime}}
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit, then they are both in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
or both are not in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
. Thus

(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|}=(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid i<0\}|},

i.e.
|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|\equiv|\{(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid i<0\}|\pmod{2}
.

Conversely if
|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|\equiv|\{(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid i<0\}|\pmod{2}
, then we get that
L_{J}
and
L_{J^{\prime}}
are both in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
or both are not in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
. Because there are exactly two
SO(V)
-orbits on the set of maximal isotropic subspaces of
V
, we get that
L_{J}
and
L_{J^{\prime}}
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit.
∎

Notation 6.10
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
SO(V)
as in
3.1
where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even and let
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
be a
G^{\iota}
-orbit in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, whose Jordan type is a totally even partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
, where
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Denote by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
: the symmetric function associated respectively to
{\mathbf{c}}
. We will denote

\varXi({\mathcal{O}})=\varXi({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|

and call
\varXi({\mathcal{O}})
: the
parity of the orbit

{\mathcal{O}}
. We will say that the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
is
even
. (respectively
odd
) if
\varXi({\mathcal{O}})
is even (respectively odd).

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
G
be the reductive group
SO(V)
as in
3.1
where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is even and let
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
be two
G^{\iota}
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
, who both have the same totally even partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
corresponding to their Jordan type, where
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
are both coefficient functions
{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Denote by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and
c^{\prime}:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
: the symmetric functions associated respectively to
{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
. Then these two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
are in the same
G
-orbit if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
have the same parity, i.e. they are both even or both odd.

Proof.

Recall that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
G^{\iota}
-orbit of the element
X_{\mathbf{c}}=T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T^{-1}_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
where
E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
is defined in
3.6
and
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
is the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism defined in
3.8
. We have shown in lemma
3.9
that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
. Using the notation of the lemma
6.8
, we get easily that
T_{\mathbf{c}}(L({\mathbf{c}}))
is the maximal isotropic subspace of
V
equal to

X_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(X^{2}_{\mathbf{c}})]+X^{2}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(X^{4}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots+X^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(X^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots

and it is spanned by

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b))\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b)\text{ and }1\leq s\leq c(b)\}.

From our definition of
T_{\mathbf{c}}
, we get that this set is

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b)\text{ and }j\in J_{i}(b)\}

where
J_{i}(b)=\{1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\mid u_{i,j}=T_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{s}(b))\text{ for some $1\leq s\leq c(b)$}\}
. For all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
and
b,b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
, note that
|J_{i}(b)|=c(b)
and if
b\neq b^{\prime}
are two distinct
{\mathcal{I}}
-boxes such that
i\in Supp(b)\cap Supp(b^{\prime})
, then
J_{i}(b)\cap J_{i}(b^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Because
L({\mathbf{c}})
is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
, we get that

J({\mathbf{c}})=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b)\text{ and }j\in J_{i}(b)\}\in{\mathcal{J}}

and
T_{\mathbf{c}}(L({\mathbf{c}}))=L_{J({\mathbf{c}})}
with the notation of lemma
6.9
.

We can do the same thing for the second orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
. Now if
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit, then the two maximal isotropic subspaces
T_{\mathbf{c}}(L({\mathbf{c}}))
and
T_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}(L({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}))
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit and by lemma
6.9
, we get

\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\equiv\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c^{\prime}(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\pmod{2}.

Reciprocally assume that

\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\equiv\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c^{\prime}(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\pmod{2}.

and the two orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
are not in the same
SO(V)
-orbit, but they are in the same
O(V)
-orbit because they both have the same partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
corresponding to their Jordan type. This means that there exists
g_{3}X_{\mathbf{c}}g_{3}^{-1}=X_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
with
g_{3}\in O(V)
and
\det(g_{3})=-1
. Consequently we have that
g_{3}T_{\mathbf{c}}(L({\mathbf{c}}))g_{3}^{-1}=T_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}(L({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})
with
g_{3}\in O(V)
and
\det(g_{3})=-1
. One of these maximal isotropic subspaces, say
T_{\mathbf{c}}(L({\mathbf{c}}))
is in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
and the other
T_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}(L({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}))
is not in the
SO(V)
-orbit of
L_{J_{+}}
. By the proof of lemma
6.9
, we must have

\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\equiv 0\pmod{2}

and

\sum_{b\in{\mathbf{B}}}c^{\prime}(b)|\{i\in Supp^{top}(b)\mid i<0\}|\equiv 1\pmod{2}.

But this contradicts our hypothesis. So the two
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
are in the same
G
-orbit.

∎

6.12.

To conclude this section, we will consider the case where
|{\mathcal{I}}|
is odd and the group
G
is the reductive group
SO(V)
as in
5.2
. Because of proposition
6.4
and lemma
6.6
, we are left with the study of the
G^{\iota}
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
being either
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
or
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
as defined in proposition
5.4
(c) and its proof, whose partition corresponding to its Jordan type is the totally even partition
2^{a(2)}4^{a(4)}6^{a(6)}\dots
, where
{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathbf{B}}/\tau\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is a coefficient function
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}
. Denote by
c:{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
: the symmetric function associated respectively to
{\mathbf{c}}
. By lemma
6.6
, if
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
, then we have that
c(b)=0
whenever
\lambda(b)
is even.

Because
c(b(i,-i,0))=0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i\geq 0
, we get as proved in
4.10
that
\dim(V_{0})=\delta_{0}
is even and we can write
\delta_{0}=2\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
. Here we are using the same notation as in
4.10
.

The
G^{\iota}
-orbits
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
and
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
were defined in
4.7
and proposition
5.4
(c). Let
T_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V
be the
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism defined in
4.10
such that
\langle T_{\mathbf{c}}(u),T_{\mathbf{c}}(v)\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
. If
E_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
is the linear transformation defined in
4.7
, then
{}^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
G^{\iota}
-orbit of
X_{\mathbf{c}}=T_{\mathbf{c}}E_{\mathbf{c}}T^{-1}_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
.

Let
b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}
be an
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b^{\prime}=b(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0)
such that
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
with
i^{\prime}\geq 0\geq j^{\prime}
,
i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}>0
and
c(b^{\prime})>0
. As noted in proposition
5.4
(c), such an
{\mathcal{I}}
-box
b^{\prime}
exists. We get easily that
0\in Supp(b^{\prime})\cap Supp(\tau(b^{\prime}))
. Because
i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}>0
, then
b^{\prime}
is above the principal diagonal. Denote by
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau
: the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b^{\prime}
, i.e.
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=\{b^{\prime},\tau(b^{\prime})\}
. Consider
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
defined in the proof of proposition
5.4
(c) and denote by
E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}
: the linear transformation
E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}S^{-1}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
, then
{}^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
G^{\iota}
-orbit of
X^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}=T_{\mathbf{c}}E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}T^{-1}_{\mathbf{c}}:V\rightarrow V
.

We will now recall the notation of
3.6
.
V({\mathbf{c}})
is a vector space with its
{\mathcal{I}}
-basis

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}=\coprod_{{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbf{B}}/\tau}\coprod_{b\in{\mathcal{O}}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp(b),1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})\}

and the non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\mathbf{c}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\times V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow{\mathbf{k}}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Using the notation of
6.12
, we have the following.

(a)

0\not\in Supp^{top}(b^{\prime})
and
0\in Supp^{top}(\tau(b^{\prime}))
.

(b)

The vector subspace

{}^{\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})=E_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{2}_{\mathbf{c}})]+E^{2}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{4}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots+E^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker(E^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots

is the vector subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
spanned by the basis

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}

and this is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
.

(c)

The vector subspace

{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})=E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker((E^{\prime})^{2}_{\mathbf{c}})]+(E^{\prime})^{2}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker((E^{\prime})^{4}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots+(E^{\prime})^{k}_{\mathbf{c}}[\ker((E^{\prime})^{2k}_{\mathbf{c}})]+\dots

is the vector subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
spanned by the basis

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}

and this is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
. Moreover this last basis is equal to

\left[\left[\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{v_{i}^{j}(b)\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}\right]\setminus\{v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))\}\right]\cup\{v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime})\}.

Proof.

We can express the linear transformation
E_{\mathbf{c}}
on the elements of the basis
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote the
\langle\tau\rangle
-orbit of
b
, then, if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has no overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i>0$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i<0$;}\end{cases}

while if the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has overlapping
{\mathcal{I}}
-box supports,
b=b(i_{1},j_{1},k_{1})\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
1\leq j\leq{\mathbf{c}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
i\in Supp(b)
, then we have

E_{\mathbf{c}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))=\begin{cases}{\phantom{-}}0,&\text{if $i=\max(Supp(b))$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i\neq\max(Supp(b))$ and $i>0$;}\\
-v_{i+2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i<0$;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i=0$, $\max(Supp(b))>0$ and $b$ is below the principal}\\
&\text{diagonal;}\\
{\phantom{-}}v_{2}^{j}(b),&\text{if $i=0$, $\max(Supp(b))>0$ and $b$ is above the principal}\\
&\text{diagonal;}\end{cases}

Note that if
b\in{\mathbf{B}}
is such that
c(b)>0
, then
b
is not on the principal diagonal as noted in
6.12

(a) We have that
b^{\prime}=b(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0)
with
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i^{\prime}\geq 0\geq j^{\prime}
and
(i^{\prime}+j^{\prime})>0
. Because the elements of
{\mathcal{I}}
are even integers, we have that
(i^{\prime}+j^{\prime})\geq 2
. We have that
i^{\prime\prime}\in Supp^{top}(b^{\prime})
if and only if

i^{\prime}\geq i^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{(i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}+2)}{2}\geq 2\quad\text{ and }\quad 0\not\in Supp^{top}(b^{\prime}).

We have that
\tau(b^{\prime})=b(-j^{\prime},-i^{\prime},0)
. We have that
i^{\prime\prime}\in Supp(\tau(b^{\prime}))
if and only if

-j^{\prime}\geq i^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{(-j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}+2)}{2}.

But
0\geq j^{\prime}
implies that
-j^{\prime}\geq 0
.
(i^{\prime}+j^{\prime})\geq 2
implies that
0\geq(-j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}+2)
and
0\in Supp^{top}(\tau(b^{\prime}))
.

(b) The proof is similar to the proof in lemma
6.8
and is left to the reader.

(c) We have that
(E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}})^{r}=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}^{r}S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}^{-1}
for all integers
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. From this, we get easily that

\ker((E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}})^{2k})=\ker(S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}^{2k}S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}^{-1})=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(\ker(E_{\mathbf{c}}^{2k}))

and

(E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}})^{k}[\ker((E^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}})^{2k})]=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}E_{\mathbf{c}}^{k}S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}^{-1}[S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(\ker(E_{\mathbf{c}}^{2k}))]=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(E_{\mathbf{c}}^{k}[\ker(E_{\mathbf{c}}^{2k})])

Thus
{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})=S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}({}^{\prime}L({\mathbf{c}}))
. Because
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}:V({\mathbf{c}})\rightarrow V({\mathbf{c}})
is an
{\mathcal{I}}
-graded isomorphism such that

\langle S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(u),S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(v)\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}=\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbf{c}}
for all
u,v\in V({\mathbf{c}})
, then
{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V({\mathbf{c}})
and

\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{\mathbf{B}}\\
c(b)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(v_{i}^{j}(b))\mid i\in Supp^{top}(b),1\leq j\leq c(b)\}

is a basis of
{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
.

For the latter description of this basis, we need to note that
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}
is the identity on all the vectors except for
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime})
and
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))
. By (a),
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime})\not\in{}^{\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
and
v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))\in{}^{\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
. Thus we get that
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime}))=v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))\not\in{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
and
S_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}}(v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(\tau(b^{\prime}))=v_{0}^{c(b^{\prime})}(b^{\prime})\in{}^{\prime\prime}L({\mathbf{c}})
. This concludes the proof of (c).
∎

The following lemma is probably well-known. Its proof is included for completeness.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be odd and using the notations of
5.2
and
6.12
, consider the set
{\mathcal{J}}
of subsets
J
of
\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
such that

•

if
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j)\in J
are such that
i,i^{\prime}\neq 0
, then
i+i^{\prime}\neq 0
and

•

if
(0,j),(0,j^{\prime})\in J
, then
j+j^{\prime}\neq(\delta_{0}+1)
,

and moreover
J
is maximal among such subsets.

(a)

If
J\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then
2|J|=\dim(V)
.

(b)

If
J\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then the subspace
L_{J}
of
V
spanned by
\{u_{i,j}\mid(i,j)\in J\}
is a maximal isotropic subspace of
V
.

(c)

Let
J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{J}}
. Then the two maximal isotropic subspaces
L_{J}
and
L_{J^{\prime}}
of
V
are in the same
SO(V)
-orbit if and only if

|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}\cup\{(0,j)\in J\mid\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}<j\}|\equiv\\
|\{(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid i<0\}\cup\{(0,j)\in J^{\prime}\mid\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}<j\}|\pmod{2}.

Recall that
2\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}=\delta_{0}
.

Proof.

(a) From our notation in
5.2
, we have that

\dim(V)=\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}\delta_{i}=\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}|\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}|

If
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
is such that
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
, then, by the maximality of
J
, we have if
i\neq 0
, that exactly one and only one of
(i,j)
,
(-i,j)
belongs to
J
, while if
i=0
, exactly one and only one of
(0,j)
,
(0,(\delta_{0}+1-j))
belongs to
J
. Consequently
2|J |=\dim(V)
.

(b) First we will show that
L_{J}
is isotropic. From our hypothesis on the basis as defined in
5.2
, we have that
\langle u_{i,j},u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\rangle\neq 0
if and only if either
i,i^{\prime}\neq 0
,
j=j^{\prime}
and
i+i^{\prime}=0
or
i=i^{\prime}=0
,
j+j^{\prime}=(\delta_{0}+1)
when
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
are such that
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\delta_{i^{\prime}}
. But by our hypothesis on
J
, when
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in J
, then
\langle u_{i,j},u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\rangle=0
. Otherwise we would have either
i,i^{\prime}\neq 0
,
j=j^{\prime}
and
i+i^{\prime}=0
or
i=i^{\prime}=0
and
j+j^{\prime}=\delta_{0}
. But this is impossible for two elements of
J
. So
L_{J}
is isotropic. Because
2\dim(L_{J})=\dim(V)
by (a), we get that
L_{J}
is maximal.

(c) We first need to remind some facts. Let

J_{+}=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid i>0,1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}\cup\{(0,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}\}.

Clearly
J_{+}\in{\mathcal{J}}
. Note that if
g\in O(V)
is such that
g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J_{+}}
, then
g\in SO(V)
. In fact by considering the matrix of
g
in the basis
\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in{\mathcal{I}},1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
ordered properly, i.e. the basis elements corresponding to the indexes in
J_{+}
and then the one corresponding to the indexes in the complement of
J_{+}
in
\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
in the right order, we get that this matrix is of the form

\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ such that }\quad\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}^{T}\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}.

Consequently
A^{T}D=I
,
\det(D)=(\det(A))^{-1}
and
g\in SO(V)
.

Let
g_{J}:V\rightarrow V
denote the unique linear transformation such that

g_{J}(u_{i,j})=\begin{cases}u_{i,j},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\in J$:}\\
u_{-i,j},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$;}\\
u_{i,j},&\text{if $i=0$, $1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(i,j)\in J$;}\\
u_{i,(\delta_{0}+1-j)},&\text{if $i=0$, $1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$;}\\
u_{-i,j},&\text{if $i<0$ and $(i,j)\in J$;}\\
u_{i,j},&\text{if $i<0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$;}\\
u_{i,(\delta_{0}+1-j)},&\text{if $i=0$, $(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)\leq j\leq\delta_{0}$ and $(i,j)\in J$;}\\
u_{i,j},&\text{if $i=0$, $(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)\leq j\leq\delta_{0}$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

We can now show that
g_{J}\in O(V)
. For this, we have to consider the following two situations:

•

\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle
whenever
i_{1},i_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}
, with
i_{1},i_{2}\neq 0
,
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{i_{1}}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq\delta_{i_{2}}
;

•

\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle
whenever
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{0}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq\delta_{0}
.

We will now consider the first case:
i_{1},i_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}
with
i_{1},i_{2}\neq 0
,
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{i_{1}}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq\delta_{i_{2}}
, we must consider
\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2})}\rangle
. We have

\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $j_{1}=j_{2}$ and $i_{1}+i_{2}=0$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=0,

where
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
and
i^{\prime}_{2}\in\{i_{2},-i_{2}\}
, because
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.

If
j_{1}=j_{2}
and
i_{1}+i_{2}=0
, then we can assume by symmetry that
i_{1}>0
and
i_{2}=-i_{1}<0
. Moreover
(i_{1},j_{1})\in J
if and only if
(i_{2},j_{2})\not\in J
. Thus

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\begin{cases}\langle u_{i_{1},j_{1}},u_{i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle,&\text{if $(i_{1},j_{1})\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{-i_{1},j_{1}},u_{-i_{2},j_{2}}\rangle,&\text{if $(i_{1},j_{1})\not\in J$;}\end{cases}\quad=1.

If
j_{1}=j_{2}
,
i_{1}+i_{2}\neq 0
and
i_{1}-i_{2}\neq 0
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{2},j_{2}}\rangle=0

because
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
,
i^{\prime}_{2}\in\{i_{2},-i_{2}\}
and
i^{\prime}_{1}+i^{\prime}_{2}\neq 0
from our hypothesis.

Finally if
j_{1}=j_{2}
,
i_{1}+i_{2}\neq 0
and
i_{1}=i_{2}
, then

\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{2},j_{2}})\rangle=\langle g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{i_{1},j_{1}})\rangle=\langle u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}},u_{i^{\prime}_{1},j_{1}}\rangle=0

where
i^{\prime}_{1}\in\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}
.

We will now consider the second case. If
1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq\delta_{0}
, then we must consider
\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle
and
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle
. We have

\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if $j_{1}+j_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}

If
\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle=1
, we cannot have both
j_{1}\geq(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)
and
j_{2}\geq(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)
, because otherwise
(j_{1}+j_{2})\geq(\delta_{0}+2)>(\delta_{0}+1)
. Similarly we cannot have both
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
, because otherwise
(j_{1}+j_{2})\leq 2\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}<(\delta_{0}+1)
. Consequently we can assume, using symmetry, that
1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
and
(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)\leq j_{2}\leq\delta_{0}
when
\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle=1
.

So when
\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle=1
, i.e
(j_{1}+j_{2})=(\delta_{0}+1)
, we have that
(0,j_{1})\in J
if and only if
(0,j_{2})=(0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1}))\not\in J

g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}})=\begin{cases}u_{0,j_{1}},&\text{if $(0,j_{1})\in J$;}\\
u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})},&\text{if $(0,j_{1})\not\in J$;}\end{cases}

and

g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})=\begin{cases}u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{2})},&\text{if $(0,j_{2})\in J$;}\\
u_{0,j_{2}},&\text{if $(0,j_{2})\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

Thus

\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=\begin{cases}\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle&\text{if $(0,j_{1})\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{0,\delta_{0}+1-j_{1}},u_{0,\delta_{0}+1-j_{2}}\rangle&\text{if $(0,j_{1})\not\in J$;}\end{cases}\quad=1

because
j_{1}+j_{2}=\delta_{0}+1
.

If
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{0,j^{\prime}_{1}},u_{0,j^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle=1
, where
j^{\prime}_{1}\in\{j_{1},(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})\}
and
j^{\prime}_{2}\in\{j_{2},(\delta_{0}+1-j_{2})\}
, then
j^{\prime}_{1}+j^{\prime}_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)
. We thus have four cases to consider: either
j^{\prime}_{1}=j_{1}
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=j_{2}
with
j_{1}+j_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)
or
j^{\prime}_{1}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{2})
with
j_{1}+j_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)
or
j^{\prime}_{1}=j_{1}
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{2})
with
j_{1}=j_{2}
or
j^{\prime}_{1}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=j_{2}
with
j_{1}=j_{2}
.

If
j_{1}=j_{2}
, then we get that

\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=\begin{cases}\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{1}}\rangle,&\text{if $1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(0,j_{1})\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})},u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})}\rangle,&\text{if $1\leq j_{1}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(0,j_{1})\not\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})},u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})}\rangle,&\text{if $(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{0}$}\\
&\text{and $(0,j_{1})\in J$;}\\
\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{1}}\rangle,&\text{if $(\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}+1)\leq j_{1}\leq\delta_{0}$}\\
&\text{and $(0,j_{1})\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

So if
j_{1}=j_{2}
, we get that
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=0
contrary to our hypothesis that
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=1
.

We are left with the two cases: either
j^{\prime}_{1}=j_{1}
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=j_{2}
with
j_{1}+j_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)
or
j^{\prime}_{1}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{1})
,
j^{\prime}_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1-j_{2})
with
j_{1}+j_{2}=(\delta_{0}+1)
. Thus we have that
\langle g_{J}(u_{0,j_{1}}),g_{J}(u_{0,j_{2}})\rangle=\langle u_{0,j_{1}},u_{0,j_{2}}\rangle=1
.

This completes the proof that
g_{J}\in O(V)
. Note also that we have
g_{J}(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}
, because we only have to consider either the basis elements
u_{i,j}
for
i>0
or the basis elements
u_{0,j}
for
1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
. In the first case, if
(i,j)\not\in J
, we get that
(-i,j)\in J
and

g_{J}(u_{i,j})=\begin{cases}u_{i,j}\in L_{J},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\in J$:}\\
u_{-i,j}\in L_{J},&\text{if $i>0$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$;}\end{cases}

and, in the second case, if
(0,j)\not\in J
, we get that
(0,(\delta_{0}+1-j))\in J
and

g_{J}(u_{0,j})=\begin{cases}u_{0,j}\in L_{J},&\text{if $1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(0,j)\in J$;}\\
u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j)}\in L_{J},&\text{if $1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}$ and $(i,j)\not\in J$.}\end{cases}

We can now compute the determinant of the matrix of
g_{J}
relative to the basis
\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in{\mathcal{I}},1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}\}
where we consider first the basis elements
u_{i,j}
for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
,
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
, secondly the basis elements
u_{0,j}
for
1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
and then thirdly the basis element
u_{-i,j}
for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
i>0
,
1\leq j\leq\delta_{i}
and finally the basis elements
u_{0,(\delta_{0}+1-j)}
for
1\leq j\leq\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}
. Using the Laplace expansion of
\det(g_{J})
and using recursion, we get easily that

\det(g_{J})=(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|+|\{(0,j)\in J\mid\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}<j\}|}

If
g\in O(V)
and
g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}
, then
\det(g)=(-1)^{|\{(i,j)\in J\mid i<0\}|+|\{(0,j)\in J\mid\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}<j\}|}
, because

g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J}=g_{J}(L_{J_{+}})\quad\Rightarrow\quad g^{-1}_{J}g(L_{J_{+}})=L_{J_{+}}\quad\text{ and }\quad g^{-1}_{J}g\in SO(V)

and
